1National Astronomical Observatories, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Jia-20 Datun Road, ChaoYang District, Beijing 100012, China

ABSTRACT
Based on sensitive L-band (1.0 to 1.5 GHz) observations of pulsars using the Five-hundred-meter Aperture
Spherical radio Telescope (FAST), we analyzed single-pulse sequences from FAST-detected pulsars and identi-
ﬁed nulling, mode changing, or subpulse modulation phenomena in 374 sources. Among these, nulling has been
detected in 160 pulsars, with 127 cases reported for the ﬁrst time. Emission mode changes have been observed
in 52 pulsars, including 51 ﬁrst-time detections. Subpulse modulation has been identiﬁed in 272 pulsars, 180
of which are newly revealed, with the majority displaying subpulse drifting behavior. Subpulse drifting in some
pulsars exhibits distinct modes with varying drift properties, leading to mode changes and divergent mean pro-
ﬁles. Statistics on pulsar parameters show that pulsars exhibiting nulling and/or subpulse modulation tend to be
older, with longer periods and lower spin-down energy-loss rates. The modulation period P3 is predominantly
correlated with pulsar rotation periods, magnetic ﬁeld strengths, and spin-down energy loss.
Keywords: pulsars: general
1. INTRODUCTION
Pulsars generally emit periodic single pulses. Each pulse
within a period is termed an “individual pulse”, which con-
sists of multiple “subpulses”. The average of many individ-
ual pulses observed in a given duration is the mean pulse or
integrated pulse. For a given pulsar, the mean pulse is sta-
ble and deﬁnes the emission window in a rotation period.
From single-pulse sequences of some pulsars, three phenom-
ena have been identiﬁed: nulling, mode changing, and sub-
pulse modulation.
Nulling refers to the absence of detectable radio pulses for
one or more pulsar rotation periods. This phenomenon was
ﬁrst reported by Backer (1970a). The nulling fraction (NF,
in percentage) is deﬁned as the fraction of pulse periods in the
nulling state relative to the observed periods. Recent statis-
tics from Wang et al. (2020) indicate that over 200 pulsars
exhibit nulling behavior. It has been found that the spin-
down rates between the emission-on and -off states of PSR
B1931+24 are largely changed (Kramer et al. 2006), suggest-
ing that nulling is physically linked to changes in the magne-
tosphere, either the cessation of current or the emission mov-
ing out of sightline (Kramer et al. 2006; Wang et al. 2007;
Gajjar et al. 2012; Basu et al. 2017).
The quasi-periodic
nulling detected in some pulsars has been interpreted as the
line of sight passing through the empty region of the rotating
carousel beams (Herﬁndal & Rankin 2007; Rankin & Wright
∗E-mail: hjl@nao.cas.cn
2008; Herﬁndal & Rankin 2009; Basu et al. 2017; Wang et al.
2020).
Some pulsars display two or more quasi-stable emission
states with distinct mean proﬁles, a phenomenon known as
emission mode changing, often characterized by abrupt tran-
sitions. Changes in radio pulsar emission mode were ﬁrst
reported by Backer (1970b).
Lyne et al. (2010) demon-
strated that different emission states in certain pulsars cor-
relate with varying spin-down rates. Kou et al. (2018) ob-
served that the spin-down rate and pulse proﬁle changes
of PSR B2035+36 are triggered by glitches, i.e., the sud-
den changes of spin-down rates. However, it is widely ac-
cepted that different emission modes reﬂect distinct mag-
netospheric states (Wang et al. 2007; Lyne et al. 2010; Ng
et al. 2020). In addition, emission modes are sometimes ac-
companied by distinct polarization properties. Several pul-
sars exhibit different orthogonal polarization modes (OPMs)
across emission modes. For instance, the Q and B modes
of PSR J0946+0951 arise from the distinct contributions of
the primary polarization mode (PPM) and secondary polar-
ization mode (SPM), leading to differences in the linear po-
larization position angle curves of the integrated proﬁles of
the two emission modes (Suleymanova et al. 1998; Rankin
& Suleymanova 2006).
The interplay between emission
modes and OPM has been observed in PSR J2006−0807
(Basu et al. 2019b), J0738−4042 (Karastergiou et al. 2011),
and PSR J0742−2822 and J1107−5907 (Keith et al. 2013;
Young et al. 2014). Using FAST observation data, Yan et al.
(2023b) identiﬁed bright and weak emission states in PSRs
arXiv:2607.03047v1  [astro-ph.HE]  3 Jul 2026


## Page 2


2
J1838+1523, J1901+0510, J1909+0007, and J1929+1844,
each exhibiting distinct polarization properties.
Single pulses typically comprise one or more subpulses.
For certain pulsars, subpulses in successive rotation periods
appear at different longitudinal phases within the pulse phase
window deﬁned by the mean proﬁle, such that these sub-
pulses align along a drifting band. This subpulse drifting
phenomenon was ﬁrst discovered by Drake & Craft (1968).
The most widely accepted model for subpulse drifting is that
proposed by Ruderman & Sutherland (1975), in which drift-
ing arises from the circulation of sparks around the magnetic
axis in the polar gap due to E × B drift, leading to the circu-
lation of carousel radio emission beams. The subpulse drift-
ing is characterized by three parameters (Backer 1973): the
longitude difference in a period for two adjacent drift sub-
pulses P2, the period number for a drift-band coming back
to the same phase P3, and the drift rate D = P2/P3. There
are also many pulsars that display only temporal modulation,
without related intensity modulation on the rotation phases
(Weltevrede et al. 2006; Song et al. 2023).
Numerous studies have investigated subpulse modulation.
For example, Weltevrede et al. (2006) analyzed 187 pul-
sars observed with the Westerbork Synthesis Radio Tele-
scope (WSRT) at 21 cm and found that over 55% exhibit
subpulse drifting.
Basu et al. (2016) studied ﬂuctuation
spectra of 123 pulsars observed with the Giant Meterwave
Radio Telescope at 333 MHz and 618 MHz, categorizing
them into three groups: phase-modulated drifting, amplitude-
modulated drifting, and non-drifting pulsars. These groups
exhibit distinct distributions of spin-down energy loss rate
˙E. Phase-modulated drifting pulsars show an anti-correlation
between P3 and ˙E, supporting the “partially screened gap"
model. Basu et al. (2019a) further reﬁned this classiﬁcation
into four types: coherent phase-modulated drifting, switch-
ing phase-modulated drifting, diffuse phase-modulated drift-
ing, and low-mixed phase-modulated drifting. Song et al.
(2023) reported on the subpulse modulation properties of
1198 pulsars using MeerKAT. Subpulses of several pulsars
exhibit diverse drifting behaviors, such as reversed drift or
bi-drift, which challenge the traditional carousel circula-
tion model. Drift reversals in pulsars like PSRs B2303+30,
B0826−34, B2148+63, and B2310+42 (Redman et al. 2005;
Gupta et al. 2004; Esamdin et al. 2005; Weltevrede et al.
2006) can be explained by line-of-sight effects if the intrin-
sic drift direction remains constant. Bidirectional drifting,
where different proﬁle components exhibit opposite drift di-
rections, is attributed to coexisting inner annular and core
gaps (Qiao et al. 2004).
This phenomenon has been ob-
served in PSRs J0815+09 (McLaughlin et al. 2004; Cham-
pion et al. 2005; Szary & van Leeuwen 2017; Shang et al.
2022), B1839−3224 (Basu & Mitra 2018a). Additionally,
some pulsars exhibit non-straight drift bands (Weltevrede
et al. 2006; Basu & Mitra 2018a; Basu et al. 2019a), further
complicating theoretical models.
Many pulsars exhibit multiple phenomena in their single-
pulse sequences. Some pulsars display subpulse drifting in
two or more distinct modes with different drift rates (e.g.,
Deich et al. 1986; Hankins & Wolszczan 1987; van Leeuwen
et al. 2002; Redman et al. 2005; Kloumann & Rankin 2010;
Rankin et al. 2013; Basu & Mitra 2018b; Rahaman et al.
2021, etc). Some pulsars show different emission or drifting
modes separated by nulls (Redman et al. 2005; van Leeuwen
et al. 2002; Wang et al. 2007; Kloumann & Rankin 2010;
Rankin et al. 2013; Basu & Mitra 2018b). Lyne & Ashworth
(1983) showed that the drifting rate of PSRs B0809+74 and
B0818−13 changes at the end of emission and then relaxes
back exponentially to the normal rate after null starts, with
the timescale proportional to the null length.
The phases
of drifting subpulses of PSRs B0031−07 and J1840−0840
are related before and after nulls (Vivekanand & Joshi 1997;
Gajjar et al. 2017). Related to drifting subpulses, the inten-
sities at given longitude phases often show regular ﬂuctua-
tions, which can be analyzed by the ﬂuctuation spectra (e.g.,
Backer 1970c; Edwards & Stappers 2002; Weltevrede et al.
2006; Song et al. 2023).
Numerous statistical studies have been conducted on
single-pulse phenomena. Ritchings (1976) and Weltevrede
et al. (2006) demonstrated that pulse nulling and subpulse
drifting, respectively, are characteristics of older pulsars.
Wang et al. (2007) further reported that the nulling fraction
correlates more strongly with pulsar age than with its spin
period. Ng et al. (2020) found that nulling is linked to both
pulsar period and age, with long-period old pulsars exhibit-
ing a higher likelihood of nulling, while mode-changing is
predominantly associated with longer-period pulsars. Wol-
szczan (1980) and Rankin (1986) reported the correlations
between P3 (the drift cycle period) and the pulsar spin-down
energy loss rate ˙E, age, and pulsar magnetic ﬁelds. Basu
et al. (2016, 2019a) conﬁrmed an anti-correlation between ˙E
and P3.
Since 2019, we have been using the Five-hundred-meter
Aperture Spherical radio Telescope (FAST, Nan 2006) to
conduct the “Galactic Plane Pulsar Snapshot (GPPS) survey”
(Han et al. 2021, 2025) and other projects, such as probing
magnetic ﬁelds in the Galactic halo through pulsar polar-
ization observations (Xu et al. 2022). These long-term ef-
forts have yielded a substantial dataset for numerous pulsars
(Wang et al. 2023a; Jing et al. 2025). Owing to the exception-
ally high sensitivity of the FAST, the pulse sequences of more
pulsars exhibit nulling, mode changing, or subpulse modula-
tion phenomena. In this paper, we analyze 374 pulsars ob-
served with FAST (see Table 1), excluding some sources al-
ready published using FAST data, such as PSRs B1859+07
(Wang et al. 2023b), B1929+10 (Kou et al. 2021), B2016+28
(Lu et al. 2019), and B1946+35 (Chang et al. 2025). The ob-
servational and analytical methods for studying nulling and
subpulse modulation are described in Section 2.
In Sec-
tion 3, we discuss the nulling, mode-changing, or subpulse
modulation phenomena in both previously known and newly
discovered pulsars.
Section 5 presents statistical analyses
of these pulsars, including parameter distributions related to
these phenomena and correlations between nulling fraction
or subpulse modulation period P3 and pulsar parameters, and
also provides conclusions.

2. FAST OBSERVATIONS AND ANALYSIS METHODS
Pulsar observations with FAST, conducted as part of the
GPPS survey key project (Han et al. 2021) or other co-
authors’ free-applied projects, together with the opened
archived FAST data, all utilize the L-band 19-beam receiver
(Jiang et al. 2020). This receiver covers a frequency band
of 1.0 to 1.5 GHz and has a system temperature of approxi-
mately 22 K. Digital backend systems record data for 1024,
2048, or 4096 channels across one or all 19 beams, with each
channel capturing either four polarization products (XX,
Y Y , Re[X∗Y ] and Im[X∗Y ]). The data sampling rate is
consistently 49.152 µs, and recorded data are stored in FITS
ﬁles.
When a pulsar is detected in any of the 19 beams, the
recorded data are folded into a single-pulse sequence us-
ing previously obtained pulsar ephemeris which were often
taken from the pulsar catalog Manchester et al. (2005). We
generally update the dispersion measure and period from
the new FAST observations by using DSPSR (van Straten

& Bailes 2011). Subsequently, PSRCHIVE software (Hotan
et al. 2004) is employed to remove radio frequency interfer-
ence (RFI) from the data and correct for Faraday rotation
when four polarization channels are recorded. Following the
polarization calibration procedure described in Wang et al.
(2023a), we obtain the total power pulse proﬁle or polariza-
tion proﬁles of pulsars for which data with four polarization
channels are recorded. The new rotation measure (RM) is ob-
tained via RMFIT (Hotan et al. 2004). The Faraday rotation
of linear polarization is corrected, and the ﬁnal polarization
proﬁles are obtained independently for each mode discussed
below. To generate a high-quality single-pulse sequence, the
baseline is typically ﬁtted with a polynomial function in off-
pulse regions and subtracted. In total, we get detailed single-


## Page 11


11
pulse sequences of 374 pulsars observed by FAST, see Table
1 for a summary.
3. VARIOUS BEHAVIORS OF INDIVIDUAL PULSES
OF PULSARS REVEALED BY FAST
Analyzing single-pulse sequences can uncover phenomena
such as nulling, mode changing, and subpulse modulation.
FAST observations in general lasted for short duration, ex-
cept for some pulsars with longer observation time. Short
FAST observations can qualitatively reveal the various be-
haviors of individual pulses owing to the high sensitivity,
but quantitative statistics of some individual pulse properties
(such as the nulling fraction) are meaningful only from long
FAST observations.
Among the 374 pulsars observed by FAST, we ﬁnd from
available data that 70 pulsars show merely nulling phe-
nomenon, 31 pulsars show different modes in our observa-
tions, and 170 pulsars only show subpulse modulation. Some
pulsars show two kinds of behaviors: a pulsar has nulling and
mode changing; 82 pulsars show nulling and modulation; 13
pulsars show different emission modes related to subpulse
modulation; 7 pulsars show nulling, mode changing and also
modulation. We have summarized these behaviors in the Ta-
ble 1. In the following, we will discuss the general methods
that will be applied to each pulsar and work on each kind of
these behaviors in a subsection.
3.1. Nulling merely
From the sensitive FAST observations, we found 70 pul-
sars with merely nulling behavior as listed in Table 1, and
59 of them have the nulls detected for the ﬁrst time. The
references are given in Table 1 for pulsars with previously
revealed nulling. Furthermore, the time-limited FAST obser-
vations did not detect nulling for several previously known
nulling pulsars, such as PSRs J0540+3207 (subsection 4.16),
J0629+2415 (4.28) and J1921+2153 (4.248). Some previ-
ously known nulling pulsars, such as PSR J0540+3207 (see
Subsection 4.16), exhibit a weak emission state instead of a
nulling state in the FAST data. Longer observations prob-
ably can reveal more nulling pulsars, known or previously
unknown.
The presence of pulse nulling can be directly observed in
single-pulse sequences. In general analyses, we ﬁrst compute
the average proﬁle to deﬁne the on-pulse window. Subse-
quently, we conduct a statistical analysis of the pulse energy.
The on-pulse energy distribution histogram is compared with
that of an off-pulse window of the same size. Nulling is in-
dicated by an additional distinct peak around zero in the on-
pulse energy distribution, in addition to the typical on-pulse
energy histogram. The nulling fraction (NF) can be calcu-
lated from these two peaks in the on-pulse energy distribution
(e.g. Wang et al. 2007; Basu et al. 2017; Wang et al. 2020).
Typically, the energy distribution of the off-pulse window can
be approximated by a Gaussian function, as it results from
random ﬂuctuations. This Gaussian function is characterized
by an amplitude Aoﬀand a width σoﬀ. If there is a peak
around zero in the on-pulse energy distribution, we can ﬁt

Figure 7. Averaged intensity proﬁles (I, solid line), the linear po-
larization proﬁles (L, dashed line) and the circular polarization pro-
ﬁles (V , dotted line) for the weak (red) and bright (blue) emission

where δAon and δAoﬀrepresent the uncertainties obtained
from the ﬁtting. When the emission and null distributions are
well separated, NF reﬂects the true nulling fraction. In cases
where there is a signiﬁcant overlap of two distributions, the
NF estimate tends to represent an upper limit. Ideally, when
the pulse sequence is long enough, we should determine the
duration and frequency of nulling events. Moreover, if the
observation duration is insufﬁcient, the nulling fraction de-
rived from a short pulse sequence may not accurately repre-
sent the true nulling properties of a pulsar.
The simple “nulling fraction” is inadequate for a compre-
hensive description of nulling. For instance, given a nulling
fraction of 40%, we cannot determine which 400 periods out
of 1000 are in a nulling state. Yang et al. (2014) and Wang
et al. (2020) introduced the “nulling degree” and “nulling
scale” to characterize nulling properties when the pulse se-
quence is of sufﬁcient length. The nulling degree is deﬁned
as the angle calculated from the durations of the nulling and
the emission states, while the nulling scale is deﬁned as the
scale period-number for consecutive nulling and emission
states.
Here we discuss one example of nulling-merely pulsars,
PSR J1501−0046.
PSR J1501−0046 was discovered by
Lynch et al. (2013) using the 100 m Robert C. Byrd Green
Bank Telescope (GBT) at 350 MHz, with a period of P =
0.4641 s. The pulsar was observed by the FAST on 20201218
for 10 minutes, over 1290 periods.
The dispersion mea-
surement (DM) determined from this observation is DM =
22.3 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences are displayed in Fig-
ure 1, which shows the nulling phenomenon. From the on-
pulse energy histogram of single pulses, evidently the dis-
tribution around zero energy is an indication for nulling.
The nulling fraction, estimated as the fraction of the on-
pulse related to the off-pulse energy Gaussian distribution,
is 48 ± 5%. According to the distributions of continuous pe-
riod numbers for adjacent nulling and emission duration (see
Figure 3), the nulling scale is estimated to be 4 ± 2 P0, and
the nulling degrees are 57 ± 18◦for nulling-emission (NE)
pairs and 58 ± 17◦for emission-nulling (EN) pairs. Accord-
ing to histograms in Figure 4, the most probable duration for
emission is 1 to 3 periods, and that for the nulling state is 1
to 4 periods, coincident with the pulse stack in Figure 1.
Note that some pulsars show partial nulling, that is the
emission absence of certain phase ranges for some periods.
During the usually-recognized nulling periods, dwarf pulses
could appear as weak narrow pulses detectable only by high
time-resolution analyses, as shown by Chen et al. (2023) for
PSR B2111+46, and later for PSR J2323+1214 (Tedila et al.
2025), PSR B1931+24 (Rusul et al. 2025), and other 10 pul-
sars (Yan et al. 2024).

61(4)
-
-
NOTE—Columns: (1) pulsar name; (2) FAST observation date (in yyyymmdd); (3)
Modulation features number (M.N) for various parts of the proﬁles: ‘W’, ‘L’, ‘C’, and
‘T’ denote the phase range of the whole proﬁle, or the leading, central, and trailing
parts of a mean pulse proﬁle, respectively. Then, the sufﬁx number stands for the
sequence number of modulation features. For instance, L.3 refers to the 3rd feature in
the leading part. For PSR J1857+0943, M.W.1 and I.W.1 denote, respectively, the ﬁrst
modulation feature of the whole proﬁle for the main pulse and interpulse (a special
case of the notation); (4) subpulse phase interval P2 in degrees, and ‘*’ for no drifting
but temporal modulation of subpulses; (5) subpulse modulation periodicity P3,
counted in pulsar period; (6) subpulse drifting rate D = P2/P3 in phase degrees per
period. Uncertainties for the columns (4-5) are in brackets; (7) Subsection for details.
sar whose mode changes had been reported previously (see
Table 1).
For pulsars exhibiting mode-changing behavior, we manu-
ally categorize emission modes from single-pulse sequences
by leveraging differences in their morphological features or
by analyzing histograms of speciﬁc characteristic parameters
(e.g., intensity of individual proﬁle components). Once emis-
sion modes for individual pulses are identiﬁed, one can com-
pare single-pulse properties of different modes and proper-
ties of mean polarization proﬁles including the polarization
position angle (PA) curves. In general, the PA curves of dif-
ferent emission modes are almost identical, with only a few
exceptions with different PA curves (Yan et al. 2023b).
Here we analyse data of PSR J1848-0023 as an example,
and detailed analyses of mode changing for other pulsars are
presented in Sect. 4.
PSR J1848-0023 was discovered during the Parkes multi-
beam pulsar survey by Hobbs et al. (2004).
The pulsar
was observed by the FAST on 20211226 for 15 minutes.
The rotation period and dispersion measurement determined
by this FAST observation are P = 0.5376 s and DM =
34.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences presented in Figure 5
show both weak and bright emission modes. In contrast to
the weak mode, the pulse intensity in the bright mode is
enhanced for all components, and the duration is relatively
shorter. In the left subpanels of Figure 5, the on-pulse en-
ergy variation is smoothed over 5 periods, showing a demar-
cation between the two modes which is detailed in the his-
togram in Figure 6, which has been used to distinguish two
emission modes. The averaged polarization proﬁles and po-
larization curves of two emission modes are shown in Fig-
ure 7. The leading component is always strongest for the two
emission modes. In the bright mode, the averaged proﬁle

the LRFS (top-left) and 2DFS approaches for the emission of the
whole pulse phase range (top-right), the Leading half part (bottom
left), and the Trailing half part (bottom right). Regular modulations
for all parts exhibit a widely enhanced ﬂuctuation spectrum with a
peak frequency around 1/P3 = 0.22, corresponding to P3 = 4.4
periods. The drifting frequency for the leading part is about P2 =
3.8 ± 0.1◦and for the trailing part P2 = 7.0 ± 0.3◦.
is relatively narrow, and the trailing component is relatively
weaker. In the weak mode, the bright leading component has
a linear polarization in the orthogonal modes, and the bridge
and trailing components have orthogonal polarization emis-
sion mixed to various extents.
Similar to PSR J1848-0023, the polarization angles of
PSRs J0302+2252 and J1922+2110 are in orthogonal modes
for different emission modes. We found that 4 pulsars, PSRs
J1909+0912, J1919+0021, J1935+1616 and J1927+1852
have different PA curves, see Sect. 4 for details.

3.3. Subpulse drifting and modulation
In general, continuous drifting over all proﬁle components
has the same modulations for all subpulses. The subpulse
drifts towards the later rotation phase in the emission win-
dow, which is a positive drift with a drifting rate D > 0,
and the drifting to an earlier phase in the next period is the
negative drift with a rate of D < 0. For some pulsars, the
subpulses do not drift in phase (D ∼0), but pulse intensities
periodically ﬂuctuate for one or more pulse proﬁle compo-
nents, which is the so-called temporal modulation.
From the FAST folded data, some pulsars show subpulse
drifting, but some pulsars do not show drifting but just tem-
poral modulation. We detected 170 pulsars only having sub-
pulse modulation from FAST data, and 110 of them have
the drifting or modulations detected for the ﬁrst time. For
these pulsars with previously known drifting subpulses, sen-
sitive FAST observations revealed more details. Some pul-
sars show distinct phase modulation features for different
mean proﬁle components, not necessarily co-occurring drift-
ing features.
In early days, the cross-correlation method suggested by
Sieber & Oster (1975) has been used to determine the drift

Figure 11. Similar to Fig. 9, here is ﬂuctuation analysis for data in
Fig 10 for PSR J1954+2923 observed on 20200607, with a distinct
peak around 1/P3 = 0.076 mainly caused by the trailing part.
rate D that is the longitudinal shift of subpulses in degree per
pulsar period, and the interval between adjacent subpulses
(P2, in degree of rotation phase), and also the drift cycles P3
for subpulses appearing at the same longitudes that is counted
in unites of the pulsar period. The correlation coefﬁcient K
in the cross-correlation method is calculated from the sum of
the correlations of every two successive individual pulses as
a function of phase shift p. The K −p curve is then ﬁtted by
a multi-Gaussian function, with the Gaussian center for zero
phase shift, the space between adjacent Gaussian peaks for
P2. This method is effective for a short sequence of drifting
subpulses, even with a weak signal-to-noise ratio or embed-
ded in a long non-drifting pulse sequence.

Subpulse drifting can also be analyzed via time-phase plots
or through the 1D Longitude Resolved Fluctuation Spec-
trum (LRFS, Backer 1970c; Edwards & Stappers 2002; Wel-
tevrede et al. 2006; Song et al. 2023). Single-pulse sequences
are either processed as a whole or divided into segments of
ns pulses. The results from all segments are averaged to pro-
duce a ﬁnal 2D image, with the horizontal axis for pulse lon-

trum (2DFS, Edwards & Stappers 2002) with the horizontal

the mean pulse proﬁles, we also analyze the drifting proper-
ties for different parts or components of the proﬁles for some
pulsars, with marked phase ranges.
Here we show examples of various drifting and temporal-
only modulation features: PSR J1832+2749 for a standard
subpulse drifting, PSR J1954+2923 for temporal modulation,
and PSR J0058+4950 for low-frequency modulation super-
imposed on the negative subpulse drifting.
3.3.1. PSR J1832+2749: Standard subpulse drifting
PSR J1832+2749 was discovered by Tyul’bashev et al.
(2017) using the Large Scanning Antenna of the Pushchino
Radio Astronomy Observatory at 110.25 MHz, and the ﬁnal
timing parameters were published by Olszanski et al. (2025).
This pulsar has a period of P = 0.6318 s and a dispersion
measurement of DM = 47.6 cm−3 pc, as measured by the
FAST for 5 minutes on 20211123. The single pulse sequence
is shown in Figure 8, showing a standard subpulse drifting
behavior.
We analyzed the ﬂuctuation spectra by the 1D LRFS and
2DFS as shown in Figure 9. The subpulses drifting parame-
ters are almost the same with a slight difference for the two
main proﬁle components with a power ﬂuctuation frequency
of 1/P3 = 0.22, i.e., the P3 is about 4.4 periods. However,
the subpulse intervals are slightly different in the leading part
and trailing part, with the centroid in spectra at 1/P2 = 94±4

3.3.2. PSR J1954+2923: temporal-only modulation
PSR J1954+2923 was discovered by Davies et al. (1970)
using the Mark I radio telescope at the Jodrell Bank at 408
MHz. Weltevrede et al. (2006) has studied weak single pulses
observed at the 21 cm band, and suggested the possible drift-
ing behavior of pulses for the two main components.
The FAST observation was conducted on 20200607 for 15
minutes with a much higher sensitivity. This pulsar has a
period of P = 0.4267 s and a dispersion measurement of

DM = 7.9 cm−3 pc. The pulse sequence in Fig. 10 does not
show subpulse drifting over the whole proﬁle or each of the
two primary components. The LRFS and 2DFS analyses of
leading and trailing parts in the pulse window show different
pulse energy ﬂuctuations, as displayed in Figure 11, but no
drifting feature. The leading part exhibits a wide red-noise-
like ﬂuctuation feature, while the trailing part has a distinct
ﬂuctuation feature with a centroid around 1/P3 = 0.0746 ±
0.0001, corresponding to P3 = 13.40 ± 0.02 periods.
3.3.3. Low-frequency modulation superimposed on subpulse
drifting
Multiple modulation features of some pulsars appear dif-
ferent components, and some exhibit low-frequency modula-
tion in addition to the fast subpulse modulation feature, prob-
ably caused by the rotating subbeam carousel cycle. PSR
J1857+0057 has been reported to have a low-frequency mod-
ulation of about every 50 periods (Yan et al. 2023a). Hsu
et al. (2025) presented the coexistence of periodic drifting
subpulses and rapid amplitude modulation observed in PSR
J1514−4834.
From
our
FAST
observations,
4
pulsars,
PSRs
J0058+4950, J1835-0149g, J1857+0057 and J2340+08, are
found to exhibit a low-frequency modulation in addition
to subpulse drifting or modulations.
The details of PSR
J1857+0057 have been reported by Yan et al. (2023a), which
has a low-frequency modulation of about every 50 periods on
pulse intensity and drift parameters. For PSRs J0453+1559
and J0621+1002, two proﬁle components show a similar
modulation periodicity, but a low-frequency modulation oc-
curs only for one component.
Here we present the results for PSR J0058+4950 in the
main text and the data of the others in the Sect.4.
PSR J0058+4950 was discovered by Stovall et al. (2014)
using the Green Bank Telescope at 350 MHz. Single pulses

3.4. Nulling and subpulse modulation
FAST observations identiﬁed 82 pulsars exhibiting both
nulling and subpulse modulation behaviors. Their nulling pe-
riod numbers have been given in Table 1, while the drifting
parameters are given in Table 3. Among them, the nulling
phenomenon is revealed for the ﬁrst time for 61 pulsars, and
subpulse drifting is newly identiﬁed for 58 pulsars. Here
we analyse PSR J1920+1040 with both nulling and subpulse
drifting behaviors, as an example. The data of other pulsars
are presented in the Sect. 4.

Figure 19. Similar to Figure 7, but for the polarization proﬁles
for slow drifting mode and fast drifting mode of PSR J2051+4434g
from FAST observation on 20230525.
PSR J1920+1040 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004). With a subintegration time
of 10 seconds, Wang et al. (2007) estimated the nulling frac-
tion to be ∼50% from a 2-hour observation by Parkes, but
no single pulse and hence no drifting was ever detected after
such a subintegration.
The pulsar was observed three times by FAST: a 15-
minute observation on 20220113, and two 5-minute obser-
vations on 20210130 and 20240106. Figure 14 displays sin-
gle pulse sequences of three observations. The rotation pe-
riod and dispersion measurement from FAST observations
are P = 2.2158 s and DM = 307.2 cm−3 pc, respec-
tively. Both the on-pulse energy histograms in Figure 15 and
the period numbers in Table 1 give the nulling fraction of
this pulsar to be 32±5%. In the emission mode, the sub-
pulses are drifting in the one-component phase window. The
LRFS and 2DFS analyses of FAST data on 20220113 (Fig-
ure 16) exhibit the drifting feature for 1/P2 = 46.8±0.8 and
1/P3 = 0.362 ± 0.001, corresponding to P3 = 2.77 ± 0.01
periods and P2 = 7.7 ± 0.1◦.
3.5. Mode changing and subpulse drifting
FAST observations revealed different emission modes for
13 pulsars (see Table 1) with at least one mode for subpulse
drifting. We present data of PSR J2051+4434g here, which
has two drifting modes. Data of other pulsars are presented
in the Sect. 4.
PSR J2051+4434g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey by Han et al. (2021), with the longest observation by
FAST on 20230525 for 88 minutes.
The rotation period
and dispersion measurement from this FAST observation are
P = 1.3031 s and DM = 613.02 cm−3 pc, respectively.
Two single pulse segments from this observation are shown
in Fig. 17, which display two drifting modes. The slow drift-
ing mode dominates and appears in most of the observation
time, as labelled by red bars in the ﬁgure. The fast drift-
ing mode occupies much shorter durations, as labelled by
the green bars. Drifting parameters are derived from fea-

tures in 2DFS of two drifting modes (Fig. 18), as listed in
Table 1. For the fast drifting mode, the modulation frequen-
cies of the leading component are 1/P3 = 0.063 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −8.7 ± 0.5, yielding P3 = 15.9 ± 0.2 periods
and P2 = −41 ± 2◦. The trailing component displays values
of 1/P3 = 0.060 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −7.7 ± 0.4, corre-
sponding to P3 = 16.8 ± 0.2 periods and P2 = −47 ± 2◦.
The slow drifting mode is characterized by longer tempo-
rally modulated periodicities: the leading component has
1/P3 = 0.0190 ± 0.0003 and 1/P2 = −12.3 ± 0.6, yielding
P3 = 52.6±0.8 periods and P2 = −29±1◦; and the trailing
component has a drifting feature of 1/P3 = 0.0187±0.0002
and 1/P2 = −9.6±0.4, which correspond to P3 = 53.4±0.7
periods and P2 = −37 ± 1◦. As shown in Figure 19, the av-
eraged polarization proﬁles are almost identical for the two
drifting modes.
3.6. Pulsars with nulling, mode changing and subpulse
drifting
From FAST observations, we found that 7 pulsars,
PSRs J1610+1322, J1822+1120, J1919+0134, J1916+1023,
J1927+1852, J1941+4320 and J1941+4320, have nulling,
mode changing and subpulse drifting phenomena revealed
in the pulse sequence. Here we present the features of PSR
J1927+1852 and leave the other 6 pulsars in the Sect. 4.
PSR J1927+1852 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
Hulse & Taylor (1975). Song et al. (2023) reported two drift-
ing features for components. J1927+1852 was observed by
the FAST on 20200401 for 15 minutes, and we got the ro-
tation period of P = 0.4827 s and a dispersion measure-
ment of DM = 264.5 cm−3 pc from our observation. The
single pulse sequences from FAST observation show much
more detail, as shown in Figure 20. In addition to the two
regular drifting modes, the slow and fast drifting modes, the
nulling or varying drifting emerges for some periods as the
transitions between the two drifting modes. The drift bands
of the slow drifting mode are very clear, showing a domi-
nating leading proﬁle component. The ﬂuctuation analyses
of the LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 21 give the drifting feature
of 1/P3 = 0.1565 ± 0.0004 and 1/P2 = −40.4 ± 0.7,
corresponding to P3 = 6.39 ± 0.01 periods and P2 =
−8.9 ± 0.2◦. However, the drift bands of the fast drifting
mode are less stable, sometimes with emission in the cen-
tral proﬁle phase screened for some periods. The ﬂuctuation
analysis of the LRFS and 2DFS give a wide frequency fea-
tures around 1/P3 = 0.402 and 1/P2 = −24.6, correspond-
ing to P3 = 2.49 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −14.6 ± 0.5◦.
There are transition periods between the fast and slow
drifting modes, such as the period numbers 225 to 235, 1294
to 1304, and 1492 to 1518 in Figure 20. The drift bands
appear to change the drifting rates gradually from the slow
drifting mode to the fast drifting mode. In some periods, the
subpulses are very disordered or chaotic, such as between the
period numbers of 280 to 300, 560 to 600, and 1520 to 1530.
Generally, nulling appears after the end of fast drifting
mode or for reorganizing the disordered drifting subpulses,
for example, around period numbers of 465, 1205 and 1563.
To analyse the nulling phenomenon, we plot the on-pulse en-
ergy distribution from the FAST observation conducted on
20200411, as shown in Figure 22. The distribution around
the zero energy indicates the nulling, with a nulling fraction
of about 1.7 ± 0.2%.
Fig. 23 shows the average polarization proﬁles for the slow
and fast drifting modes. The leading component of the slow
drifting mode is much brighter than that in the fast drifting
mode, and its linear polarization angles are in the orthogonal
polarization modes (OPM) with a 90◦jump from the smooth
average PA curve of the other part. In contrast, the PA curve
of the fast drifting mode is a continuously monotonically de-
creasing function over all proﬁle components. This implies
the correlation between emission modes and orthogonal po-
larization modes, as previously found in PSRs J2006-0807
and J1239+2453 (Basu et al. 2019a; Smith et al. 2013).
4. DETAILS OF INDIVIDUAL PULSES FOR 366
PULSARS
In addition to the examples in the last Section, we present
details for prominent features of individual pulses of 366 pul-
sars here. Hereafter, except for some speciﬁc cases remarked
in the captions, ﬁgures in this section have the same format
as in the previous section, i.e. all single pulse sequences are
plotted in the same form as Fig. 1, and the pulse energy dis-
tribution in the same form as Fig. 2, and the LRFS and 2DFS
analyses in the same form as Fig. 9, polarization proﬁles in
the same form as Fig. 7. Relevant parameters have been pre-
sented in the tables for various features in Sect. 3.

All-Sky Survey (LOTAAS) (Sanidas et al. 2019).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210927 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.5367 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 53.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 24 and ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 25 illustrate the exis-

4.2. J0115+6325
PSR J0115+6325 was discovered in the LOFAR Tied-
Array All-Sky Survey (LOTAAS) (Sanidas et al. 2019).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250811 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.5214 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 65.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No.1-400 are shown
in Fig. 26. From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 27, there are
a negative drift feature and a temporal modulation feature
in 2DFS. For the negative drift feature, the frequency 1/P3
ranges widely from 0.14 to 0.5. The centroid frequencies
of the drift feature are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.28 ± 0.01
and 1/P2 = −48 ± 1, corresponding to drift periodicities
of P3 = 3.6 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −7.5 ± 0.1 de-
grees. The centroid frequency of the modulation feature is
1/P3 = 0.100 ± 0.002, yielding P3 = 10.0 ± 0.2.

ing mode and fast drifting mode of PSR J0302+2252 from the observation

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201101, with
a rotation period P = 1.2071 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 19.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences of this ob-
servation in Fig. 35 illustrate the intensity variations as re-
ported by the previous studies. From the tripodal shape of
the on-pulse integral energy histogram of individual pulses
in Fig. 35, weak, normal, and bright emission modes are dis-
tinguished, which are labeled by red, green, and yellow, re-
spectively. Averaged polarization proﬁles and PA curves of
these three modes are displayed in Fig. 37. From PA tracks
of different emission modes, the weakest mode is dominated
by the secondary polarization mode, while normal and bright
modes are dominated by the primary polarization mode, re-
sulting in a PA difference of about 90◦between the weak
mode and the other two modes. For the leading and trailing
parts in the proﬁle, the sign of circular polarization of the
weak mode is also opposite to that of the other two emission
modes.

4.8. J0453+1559
PSR J0453+1559 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
at 327 MHz (Deneva et al. 2013).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210108 for 9 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.0458 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 30.4 cm−3 pc from this observation.
Single-pulse sequences of PSR J0453+1559 are displayed in
Fig. 41. Because of the very weak emission in the trailing
part of the mean proﬁle (Wang et al. 2023a), we refer to the
phase ranges related to the two brighter regions as “leading"
and “central" parts, respectively. The enlarged view of pulses
No.1230-1450 in the right panel of Fig. 41 illustrates the sub-
pulse drifting for the central part of the proﬁle. From spectral
analysis of the leading part in a mean pulse proﬁle in Fig. 42,
there are two temporal modulation features: a negative drift-
ing of 1/P3 = 0.353 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −16 ± 2, yielding
P3 = 2.83 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −23 ± 3◦; and a low-
frequency modulation of 1/P3 = 0.021±0.001, which corre-
sponds to 47±2 periods. The 2DFS for the central part of the
proﬁle shows a clear drift feature of 1/P3 = 0.338 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −46±1, yielding P3 = 2.96±0.01 periods and
P2 = −7.8 ± 0.2◦.

whereas the weak mode lasts from 1 to 5 periods and typi-

PSR J0514+4009g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230213 for 15
minutes, with a rotation period P = 2.6256 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 101.0 cm−3 pc. From the single pulse
sequence in Fig. 49, the pulsar has systematic modulation be-
havior. LRFS and 2DFS are displayed in Fig. 50. The tem-
poral modulation periodicity of about 20 periods is similar
for both components. The leading component has a positive
subpulse drifting, with modulation frequencies of 1/P3 =
0.048 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 16 ± 2, yielding drifting parame-
ters of P3 = 20.7±0.3 periods and P2 = 23±3◦. While the
trailing component is more likely to be amplitude modulated

PSR J0518+2431 was discovered by FAST, and the nulling
behavior was reported in Wu et al. (2023).
The pulsar has also been observed in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey on 20220225 for 5 minutes, with a rotation period P =
2.6370 s and a dispersion measure DM = 72.5 cm−3 pc.

existence of nulls. The nulling fraction of this observation
is estimated to be 27±2% from the on-pulse integral energy
histogram in Fig. 54.

PSR J0528+2200 was discovered by Staelin & Reifen-
stein (1968). The pulsar has nulling and drifting phenom-
ena. The nulling fraction was reported to be 25% at 610 MHz
by Ritchings (1976) and at 327 MHz by Redman & Rankin
(2009), 28.31% at 327 MHz by Herﬁndal & Rankin (2009),
14.4% at 333 MHz by Basu et al. (2017), and 15.2% at 2250
MHz by Wang et al. (2020). Dwarf pulses of the pulsar have
been detected by Yan et al. (2024). Subpulse modulation was
reported by Backer (1973) and Herﬁndal & Rankin (2009) at
318 MHz and 327 MHz, and drifting behavior was reported
by Weltevrede et al. (2006, 2007) at 21 cm and 92 cm.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230913 for 49
minutes, with a rotation period P = 3.7452 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 51.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 55 display nulling and subpulse drifting be-
haviors. In the on-pulse energy histogram shown in Fig. 56,
the asymmetric distribution around zero energy is caused by
dwarf pulses (Yan et al. 2024), and the nulling fraction for
this observation is estimated to be 13±1%. From ﬂuctuation
spectra shown in Fig. 57, the leading and trailing parts in
the mean pulse proﬁle have opposite drift directions, which
is consistent with Weltevrede et al. (2006). For the leading

Figure 58. Single pulse sequences of PSR J0538+2817 from the FAST

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20190320 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.1432 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 40.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 58 show the subpulse modulation phenomenon. LRFS
and 2DFS of the leading and trailing parts in the mean pulse
proﬁle are displayed in Fig. 58. In the 2DFS of the leading
proﬁle part, there are a positive and a negative drift features
and the centroids are respectively: 1/P3 = 0.0167 ± 0.0003
(P3 = 60 ± 1 periods) and 1/P2 = 5 ± 1 (P2 = 76 ± 9 de-
grees); 1/P3 = 0.0507 ± 0.0004 (P3 = 19.7 ± 0.2 periods)
and 1/P2 = −6 ± 1 (P2 = −65 ± 9 degrees). The trailing
part exhibits a low-frequency modulation feature in 2DFS
with the centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.0336 ± 0.0003
(P3 = 29.7 ± 0.3 periods), and a negative drift feature with
the centroid at 1/P3 = 0.089 ± 0.001 (P3 = 11.2 ± 0.1 peri-
ods) and 1/P2 = −51.5 ± 0.4 (P2 = −6.99 ± 0.05 degrees).
4.16. J0540+3207
PSR J0540+3207 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope,
emitting strong, sporadic single pulses (Cordes et al. 2006).
This pulsar has been reported by Herﬁndal & Rankin (2009)
and Redman & Rankin (2009) with a nulling fraction of 53%
at 327MHz.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20191002 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.5242 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 62.1 cm−3 pc from this observation. Sin-

4.17. J0543+2329
PSR J0543+2329 was discovered by the Jodrell Bank
Mark IA radio telescope at 408 MHz (Davies et al. 1972).
This pulsar was reported to have mode changing behavior.
Nowakowski (1991) took repeated short segments of single
pulses behaving in a similar way as different modes at 430
MHz, and the typical duration is about 5 pulses. Modula-
tion behavior but no preferred drift direction were reported
by Nowakowski (1991) at 430 MHz and Weltevrede et al.
(2006, 2007) at 21 cm and 92 cm.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20190422 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.2460 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 77.5 cm−3 pc from this observation. The
modulation behavior could be conﬁrmed by FAST observa-
tion from single pulse sequences (Fig. 64) and ﬂuctuation
spectra (Fig. 65). For the modulation feature in 2DFS, the
temporal frequency of the centroid is estimated to 1/P3 =
0.080 ± 0.001, which corresponds to P3 = P3 = 12.6 ± 0.1
periods.

PSR J0546+2441 was discovered by Champion et al.
(2005) using the Arecibo telescope at 430 MHz.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20190418 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 2.8438 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 72.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse sequence in
Fig. 66 shows the nulling and negative subpulse drifting phe-
nomena. The nulling fraction is estimated to be 41±5% from
the on-pulse energy histogram (Fig. 67). The drifting param-
eters are derived from the cross-correlation method (Fig. 68),
which are P2 = 1.15 ± 0.03 degrees and D = −0.27 ± 0.05
degrees per period.
4.19. J0555+3948
PSR J0555+3948 was discovered in the Northern High
Time Resolution Universe survey using the Effelsberg radio
telescope (Barr et al. 2013). The pulsar was reported by Ng
et al. (2020) to be a possible new nulling pulsar.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210524 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 1.1468 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 35.9 cm−3 pc. From this FAST observa-
tion, the nulling behavior of the pulsar is conﬁrmed from sin-
gle pulse sequences in Fig. 69 and on-pulse integral energy
histogram in Fig. 70, with a nulling fraction estimated to be
24±2%. In addition to nulls and normal emission, there is
also weak emission, such as single pulses from No. 70 to
95, and 218 to 227, which is also consistent with that null
and pulse distributions are not well separated. Single pulse
stacks also show clear drift bands from single pulses from
No. 112 to 122.

4.20. J0611+30
PSR J0611+30 was discovered by Camilo et al. (1996a)
using the Arecibo telescope at 430 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210526 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.4119 s and a disper-

variable in the single pulse sequence.

4.21. J0612+3721
PSR J0612+3721 was discovered by Stokes et al. (1985)
with the 92-m telescope at Green Bank. Weltevrede et al.
(2006) reported the negative subpulse drifting of this pulsar.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210109 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.2980 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 27.2 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 73 show the drifting behavior of the central
component. From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 74, the central
component has a negative drift, and the leading and trailing
components seem to have temporal modulation. The drift-

4.22. J0613+3731
PSR J0613+3731 was discovered by the Low-Frequency
Array (LOFAR) (Coenen et al. 2014).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210110 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.6192 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 18.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 75 display modulation behavior. Leading, central,
and trailing phase ranges are divided in a mean proﬁle based
on the time-phase plot, and LRFS and 2DFS of these three
phase ranges are shown in Fig. 76.
For the main modu-
lation features in ﬂuctuation spectra of three phase ranges,
centroid temporal frequencies are estimated to be 1/P3 =
0.053 ± 0.001, 0.051 ± 0.001 and 0.055 ± 0.001, yielding
P3 = 19.0 ± 0.4, 19.4 ± 0.5, and 18.1 ± 0.2 periods, respec-
tively.

4.24. J0621+1002
PSR J0621+1002 was discovered using the Arecibo tele-
scope at 430 MHz Camilo et al. (1996a). Wang et al. (2021)
reported intensity modulation features in ﬂuctuation spectra
of the ﬁrst component with peak of 3.0 ± 0.1 periods, and
third pulse components with two diffused peaks of 3.0 ± 0.1
periods and 200 ± 1 periods.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200104 for 5
minutes and 20210109 for 24 minutes, with a rotation pe-
riod P
= 0.0289 s and a dispersion measure DM
=
36.5 cm−3 pc from the 24-minute observation. Single pulse
sequences and ﬂuctuation spectra of the observation on
20210109 are shown in Fig. 79 and 80.
For the leading
component, there is a hint of negative drifting with the tem-
poral modulation centroid of 1/P3 = 0.3448 ± 0.0003
(P3 = 2.88 ± 0.08 periods). There is a wide low-frequency
modulation feature with the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.084 ±
0.001 in 2DFS of the central component, corresponding to
P3 = 11.9 ± 0.1 periods. In 2DFS of the trailing compo-
nent, the drift feature is not symmetric about vertical axis,
which demonstrates negative drifting instead of periodic in-
tensity modulation. The centroid modulation frequencies are
1/P3 = 0.386 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −14.6 ± 0.2, corre-
sponding to periodicities of P3 = 2.591 ± 0.004 periods and
P2 = −24.7 ± 0.3. There is also a low-frequency modula-

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240623 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.6137 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 57.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse sequence
and a zoomed-in view of pulses No.40-200 are shown in
Fig. 81, illustrating the nulling phenomenon with a long du-
ration. The nulling fraction of this observation is estimated
to be 81.4±1.8% from the on-pulse integral energy histogram
in Fig. 82. For the emission segment between pulses No.116
and 174, LRFS and 2DFS for the central proﬁle part are dis-
played in Fig. 83, where the centroid frequency of the mod-
ulation feature is 1/P3 = 0.086 ± 0.003 (P3 = 11.7 ± 0.4
periods). More observations are required for more detailed
and accurate modulation properties.

shape corresponding to two emission modes. Two emission

To analyse the relationship between MP and IP, we set the
weak mode to be "0" and the bright mode to be "1". As a
result, two 0-1 sequences related to the occurrences of emis-
sion modes are obtained. Values of shifted cross-correlation
between 0-1 sequences of MP and IP are calculated, which
are shown in Fig. 88. On average, the correlation coefﬁcient
reaches a maximum of 0.44 when mode changes of IP are
lagged by 15 periods from MP.
Proﬁles contrast between emission modes of MP and IP
are shown in Fig. 90. For the main pulse, the emission of the
weak mode is brighter in the leading edge while weaker in the
trailing edge of the proﬁle compared to the bright mode. For
the interpulse, the emission of the weak mode is weakened,
especially in the leading part of the proﬁle, and the trailing
components are much brighter than the leading components,
which is different from the bright mode.

vation. From LRFS and 2DFS (Fig. 93) of the FAST obser-

sequence of the observation on 20240414 (Fig. 94) displays
systematic subpulse drifting behavior. From LRFS and 2DFS
in Fig. 95, two components have similar temporal modulation

for the leading component and 1/P3 = 0.167 ± 0.001
(P3 = 5.98 ± 0.03) for the trailing component. The lead-

4.30. J0646+0905
PSR J0646+0905 was discovered using the Parkes radio
telescope in the Perseus Arm Pulsar Survey (Burgay et al.
2013).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20231201 for 18
minutes and 20231212 for 17 minutes. From the observation
on 20231212, a rotation period of P = 0.9039 s and a disper-
sion measure of DM = 149.1 cm−3 pc were derived. Single
pulse sequences of this observation in Fig. 96 provide evi-
dence for the positive drifting phenomenon. In the ﬂuctuation
spectra (Fig. 97), the centroid frequencies of the positive drift
feature are 1/P3 = 0.0417 ± 0.0004 and 1/P2 = 9.4 ± 0.6,
which correspond to drifting parameters of P3 = 24.0 ± 0.2
periods and P3 = 38 ± 2◦.
4.31. J0647+0913

using the Parkes 64-m radio telescope at a central frequency
of 1374 MHz.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200113 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2350 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 156.9 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence and on-pulse integral energy histogram of the FAST
observation are shown in Fig. 98 and 99, which illustrate the

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210111 for 8 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.4284 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 44.2 cm−3 pc. We also detected sub-
pulse drifting from the FAST data. The single pulse sequence
in Fig. 104 shows organized negatively drifting bands and a
short-scale nulling phenomenon. Temporally intensity mod-
ulation frequency in LRFS and 2DFS (Fig. 106) is narrow,
indicating a systematic drift feature. Centroid modulation
frequencies of the drift feature in 2DFS are estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.162 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −74 ± 3, corresponding
to P3 = 6.19 ± 0.02 periods and P2 = −4.9 ± 0.2◦. The
nulling fraction is 4.0±0.7% from the energy histogram in
Fig. 105.

4.36. J0815+4611
PSR J0815+4611 was ﬁrst reported by Sanidas et al.
(2019) using the LOFAR Tied-Array All-Sky Survey
(LOTAAS). Zhao et al. (2023) reported the drifting properties
at 290-500 MHz using the FAST wide-band observations.
The pulsar was also observed by FAST on 20201124 for
6 minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.4342 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 10.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 110, displaying a subpulse drifting
behavior. In LRFS and 2DFS (Fig. 111), there is a drift fea-
ture with the centroid characterized by 1/P3 = 0.053±0.001
and 1/P2 = 29 ± 2, which correspond to P3 = 19.1 ± 0.3
periods and P2 = 12 ± 2◦.
4.37. J0944+4106
PSR J0944+4106 was discovered by Stovall et al. (2014)
using the Green Bank Telescope.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201124 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.2296 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 20.6 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence of the observation is displayed in Fig. 112. The his-
togram of single-pulse on-pulse integrated energy in Fig. 112
indicates the existence of nulls, and the nulling fraction of
this observation is estimated to be 8±1%. Fluctuation spec-
tra are displayed in Fig. 114, illustrate the weak drift fea-
tures for leading and trailing parts in a mean pulse pro-
ﬁle. 2DFS exhibits the centroid modulation frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.30 ± 0.01 and 1/P2 = 40 ± 10 for the leading
part of the proﬁle, corresponding to drifting parameters of
P3 = 3.4 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 9 ± 2◦. For the trailing
part of the proﬁle, the drift feature in 2DFS has the centroid
frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.313 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = 29 ± 7,
yielding P3 = 3.20 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = 12 ± 3◦.

PSR J1048+5349 was discovered by the CHIME telescope
(Dong et al. 2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240821 for 10
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.7308 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 29.2 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 115 and ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 116 il-
lustrate the existence of subpulse drifting phenomenon. In

4.41. J1502+4653
PSR J1502+4653 was discovered by FAST (Cruces et al.
2021).
This pulsar was also observed by FAST on 20250125 for
10 minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.7524 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 26.5 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation in Fig. 122 illustrate the existence
of nulling and subpulse drifting behaviors. A nulling frac-
tion is estimated from the on-pulse integral energy histogram
(Fig. 123), that is 37±4% for this observation. Drifting pa-
rameters of the leading and trailing components are obtained
from the drift features in ﬂuctuation spectra (Fig. 124). For
the leading part in a mean pulse proﬁle, the 2DFS has a neg-
ative drift feature with the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.111 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −43 ± 6, corresponding to P3 = 9.0 ± 0.1
periods and P2 = −8 ± 1◦. The negative drift feature in
2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part is characterized by 1/P3 =
0.102±0.001 and 1/P2 = −163±4, yielding P3 = 9.8±0.1
periods and P2 = −2.2 ± 0.1◦.
4.42. J1543-0620
PSR J1543-0620 was discovered by Manchester et al.
(1978) in the second Molonglo pulsar survey. Subpulse drift-
ing behavior with P3 ∼3 periods of this pulsar was reported
in previous studies. Weltevrede et al. (2006) observed oppo-
site drift directions in the two pulse proﬁle halves at 21 cm,
quantifying the phase modulation periodicity of P2 = 24+9
−4
and −16+12
−45 degrees at 21 cm. However, only positive drift-
ing was reported in the work of Weltevrede et al. (2007) with
P2 = 6.0+3
−0.5 at 92 cm, by Basu et al. (2019a) at 339 MHz,
and by Song et al. (2023) with P2 = 63+95
−60 at 1283 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250525 for 8 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.7091 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 18.1 cm−3 pc derived. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 100-200 in
Fig. 125 show the subpulse drifting phenomenon. LRFS and
2DFS are displayed in Fig. 126, and there are three drift fea-
tures in 2DFS. The negative drift feature is the most promi-

4.45. J1610-1322
PSR J1610-1322 was discovered in the Princeton-NRAO
pulsar survey with the 92 m telescope at Green Bank (Dewey
et al. 1985). Subpulse drifting with P3 = 8 ± 2 periods and
P2 = −20+5
−20 degrees were reported by Weltevrede et al.
(2007) at 92 cm.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250816 for 26
minutes, with a rotation period P = 1.0185 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 50.5 cm−3 pc determined. Single
pulse sequences in Fig. 132 illustrate the existence of the
nulling, subpulse drifting, and mode changing phenomena.
Three drifting modes are distinguished: normal, slow drift-
ing, and fast drifting modes, which are labeled with orange,
red, and green bars, respectively. The nulling fraction of this
observation is estimated to be 34±1% from the on-pulse en-
ergy histogram (Fig. 133). LRFS and 2DFS of three drift-
ing modes, obtained from their corresponding single-pulse
segments in Fig. 132, are shown in Fig. 134, and the fast
drifting mode has the smallest P3 value.
For the normal
mode, the centroid of the negative drift feature in 2DFS is
at 1/P3 = 0.136 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −28 ± 1, corre-
sponding to periodicities of P3 = 7.35 ± 0.05 periods and
P2 = −13.1 ± 0.3 degrees. The centroid of the drift fea-
ture in 2DFS of the slow drifting mode is characterized by
1/P3 = 0.043 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −29 ± 1, yielding
P3 = 23 ± 1 periods and P2 = −12.3 ± 0.4 degrees. In
2DFS of the fast drifting mode, the drift feature exhibits the

4.46. J1623-0908
PSR J1623-0908 was discovered from observations at the
Molonglo Radio Observatory and the Australian National
Radio Astronomy Observatory, Parkes (Manchester et al.
1978). Two positively drift features have been reported by
Song et al. (2023), which are P3 = 28 ± 31 periods and
P2 = −69+62
−35 degrees, and P3 = 2.5 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = 25+13
−15 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250217 for 10
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2763 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 68.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are displayed in Fig. 135, illustrating the existence
of the nulling phenomenon. From the on-pulse energy his-
togram in Fig. 136, the nulling fraction of this FAST obser-
vation is estimated to be 7.3±1.9%. However, there is no ob-
vious drift feature as previously reported in LRFS and 2DFS
(Fig. 137) from this data.
A longer observation is required for a detailed analysis of
the drifting behavior.
4.47. J1643+1338
The pulsar was ﬁrst reported by Tyul’bashev et al. (2017)
using the Big Scanning Antenna at 111 MHz.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210203 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0991 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 35.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence is displayed in Fig. 138. Drift bands are system-
atic, corresponding to the drift feature in LRFS and 2DFS
(Fig. 139).
The centroid modulation frequencies are esti-
mated to be 1/P3 = 0.361 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −68 ± 1,
yielding P3 = 2.77 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −5.3 ± 0.1◦.
Moreover, apparent drift direction seems to change around
pulse numbers 30, 100, and 460, before the single-pulse in-
tensity decreases. This may be caused by drifting direction
changes, or just drift rate changes and aliased effect (e.g.,
Weltevrede et al. 2006).

4.49. J1714-1054
PSR J1714-1054 was discovered by Jacoby et al. (2009)
with the 64 m Parkes radio telescope. The drifting param-
eters of this pulsar were reported by Song et al. (2023):
P3 = 10.2 ± 0.3 periods and P2 = 10+1
−3 degrees for one
component; P3 = 10.4 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 18+35
−6 de-
grees for the other component.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250523 for 8 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.0888 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 51.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 142. From the ﬂuctuation spec-

4.50. J1722+35
PSR J1722+35 was discovered by Tyul’bashev et al.
(2016) using the Large Phased Array at 111 MHz. The drift-
ing parameter P2 at 111 MHz was reported by Smirnova et al.
(2024) to be 117+8
−11 ms.

Figure 148. Single pulse sequence of PSR J1739+0612 from the FAST

The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211030 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.8217 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 23.6 cm−3 pc from this observation. The
single pulse sequence is shown in Fig. 144. There are three
modulation features in 2DFS (Fig. 145), two of them are re-
lated to positive drifting and one is temporally low-frequency
modulation. For positively drift features, the centroid mod-
ulation frequencies are 1/P3 = 0.265 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 =
88 ± 3 (P3 = 3.78 ± 0.04 periods and P2 = 4.1 ± 0.1◦), and
1/P3 = 0.414±0.004 and 1/P2 = 74±2 (P3 = 2.42±0.03
periods and P2 = 4.9±0.1◦). The low-frequency modulation
has 1/P3 = 0.045 ± 0.001, corresponding to P3 = 22 ± 1
periods.
4.51. J1735-0724
PSR J1735-0724 was discovered by Lyne & Large (1976).
This pulsar was reported to have the modulation behavior
with P3 of 19.7 ± 7.5 periods (Basu et al. 2016), and 13 ± 2
periods (Weltevrede et al. 2007).
Drifting parameters of
P3 = 23 ± 6 periods and P2 = 16+67
−8
degrees were also
reported by Song et al. (2023).

utes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.4193 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 73.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 146. From the ﬂuctuation spectra
in Fig. 147, there is a low-frequency modulation of 1/P3 =

−65 degrees

P2 = −129+120
−786 degrees for the trailing component.

4.53. J1741-0840
PSR J1741-0840 was discovered in the second Molonglo
pulsar survey (Manchester et al. 1978). This pulsar was re-
ported to exhibit nulling behavior by Burke-Spolaor et al.
(2012) at 1352 MHz. The nulling fraction was estimated to
be 15.7±1.7 at 333 MHz and 15.8±1.4% at 618 MHz (Basu
et al. 2017), 30±5% at 625 MHz (Gajjar et al. 2017), and
23±2% derived at 1347.3 MHz and 1369 MHz (Xu et al.
2024). Subpulse drifting and quasiperiodicity of the nulling

Figure 159. Single pulse sequences of PSR J1748-1300 from the FAST

The pulsar was discovered in a survey of intermediate
Galactic latitudes using the Parkes 64-m radio telescope (Ed-
wards et al. 2001). The positive drifting behavior of P3 =
2.61 ± 0.04 and P2 = 5.3+0.9
−0.5 degrees has been reported by
Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201128 for 8 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0455 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 89.4 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences in Fig. 156 display the sub-
pulse drifting phenomenon. In the on-pulse energy histogram
(Fig. 157), there is a distribution around zero energy that
is related to nulls. The nulling fraction of this observation
is estimated to be 1.9±0.2%. From ﬂuctuation spectra in
Fig. 158, centroid modulation frequencies of the drift fea-
ture are 1/P3 = 0.387 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 77 ± 2, which
correspond to drifting parameters of P3 = 2.582 ± 0.004 pe-
riods and P2 = 4.7 ± 0.8◦. Drifting properties are consistent
with the result of Song et al. (2023).

reported the pulsar exhibiting a amplitude modulated drifting
with P3 of 6.7 ± 0.6 periods. Song et al. (2023) presented
two features for two components, respectively: a drift feature

and the centroid frequencies are 1/P3 = 0.147 ± 0.001 and
0.146 ± 0.002, yielding P3 = 6.8 ± 0.1 and 6.9 ± 0.1 peri-
ods, respectively. In contrast, the distribution of modulation
features along the vertical axis is narrower than that of the
leading part, indicating more systematic subpulse modula-
tion.

4.58. J1750+07
PSR J1750+07 was discovered from the Arecibo 327 MHz
Drift Pulsar Survey (AO327), and was reported to have the
nulling phenomenon (Deneva et al. 2016).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210208 for 9 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 5.7266 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 56.6 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences in Fig. 163 and on-pulse inte-
gral energy histogram in Fig. 164 show the clear nulling phe-
nomenon, and the nulling fraction is estimated to be 85±17%
from this observation.
4.59. J1759-1029
PSR J1759-1029 was discovered in the mid-latitude por-
tion of the High Time Resolution Universe survey with the
Parkes 64-m radio telescope (Bates et al. 2012).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250803 for 15
minutes, with a rotation period P = 2.5124 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 115.9 cm−3 pc derived. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 165 illustrate the existence of nulling and
subpulse drifting. The nulling fraction of this observation is
estimated to be 11±1% from the on-pulse energy histogram
(Fig. 166). The ﬂuctuation spectra are displayed in Fig. 167,
and the centroid of the positive drift feature in 2DFS is at
1/P3 = 0.289 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = 35 ± 2, corresponding to

4.61. J1802+0128
PSR J1802+0128 was discovered by the Parkes 64-m radio
telescope (Edwards et al. 2001).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210117 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.1085 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 98.5 cm−3 pc from this observation.
Single pulse sequences in Fig. 171 illustrate the existence
of nulls.
The nulling fraction of this observation is esti-
mated to be 28±8% from the on-pulse integral energy his-
togram in Fig. 172. In LRFS and 2DFS (Fig. 173), there are
two weak temporally modulated features with centroids of
1/P3 = 0.054 ± 0.002 and 0.462 ± 0.002, corresponding to
P3 = 18 ± 1 and 2.16 ± 0.01 periods. The low-frequency
modulation is probably caused by the nulling behavior.
4.62. J1802-0523
PSR J1802-0523 was the High Time Resolution Universe
(HTRU) survey using the Parkes 64-m radio telescope (Bates
et al. 2012).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250910 for 10
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 5.0422 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 124.7 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence is shown in Fig. 174, illustrating the existence of
nulling and subpulse drifting behaviors. The nulling fraction
of this observation is estimated from the on-pulse energy his-
togram in Fig. 175, which is 24.3±3.6%. In ﬂuctuation spec-
tra, the centroid frequencies of the positive drift feature are
1/P3 = 0.126 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 57 ± 2, corresponding to

4.65. J1807-0847
PSR J1807-0847 was discovered by Manchester et al.
(1978) in the second Molonglo pulsar survey. Song et al.
(2023) reported a positive drifting with P3 = 3.8 ± 0.5 pe-
riods and P2 = 31+10
−16 degrees, and a negative drifting with
P3 = 4.6 ± 0.6 periods and P2 = −35+19
−6 degrees, for two
components respectively.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250319 for 6 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.1637 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 112.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No.1000-1200 in
Fig. 181 show that the modulation behavior is not system-
atic. Fluctuation spectra are displayed in Fig. 182. From
this FAST observation, the 2DFS of the on-pulse region in
a mean pulse proﬁle shows a modulation feature and a pos-
itive drift feature, whereas both positive and negative drift

4.66. J1808-0813
PSR J1808-0813 was discovered by Manchester et al.
(1996) using the Parkes radio telescope. Nulling behavior
has been reported by Weltevrede et al. (2007) at 21 cm and
by Burke-Spolaor et al. (2012) at 1352 MHz. Nulling frac-
tions at 333MHz and 618 MHz were estimated by Basu et al.
(2017) to be 12.8±1.3% and 8.2±1.0%, respectively. In ad-
dition, Song et al. (2023) presented subpulse drifting param-
eters of P3 = 2.4 ± 0.3 periods and P2 = 88+85
−80 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250319 for 6 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.8760 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 151.2 cm−3 pc derived. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation in Fig. 183 display the nulling
phenomenon, and the nulling fraction is estimated to be
16.3±1.7% from the on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 184.
While there is no obvious drift feature in the ﬂuctuation spec-
tra of this data.

4.67. J1809+17
PSR was discovered in the LOFAR Tied-Array All-Sky
Survey (LOTAAS) (Sanidas et al. 2019).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240920 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.0668 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 47.7 cm−3 pc from this observation.
The single pulse sequence is shown in Fig. 185. From ﬂuctu-
ation spectra in Fig. 186, this pulsar has a negative subpulse
drifting behavior. The centroid modulation frequencies are
estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.216±0.004 and 1/P2 = −33±3,
corresponding to drifting parameters of P3 = 4.6 ± 0.1 pe-
riods and P2 = −11 ± 1◦. The wide temporal modulation
frequency range of the drift feature indicates an unsystematic
modulation behavior.
4.68. J1809-0119
PSR J1809-0119 was discovered by Keith et al. (2010) us-
ing the Parkes Radio Telescope. The subpulse drifting with
parameters of P3 = 3.57 ± 0.05 periods and P2 = −4.1+0.2
−2
degrees was reported by Song et al. (2023).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210118 for 9 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.7450 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 138.8 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences in Fig. 187 display subpulse
drifting phenomenon. Drifting parameters are obtained from
ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 188. For the leading proﬁle part,
the centroid of the drift feature in 2DFS has modulation fre-
quencies of 1/P3 = 0.279 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −99 ± 3,
yielding P3 = 3.58 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −3.6 ± 0.1◦.
For the trailing part in a mean pulse proﬁle, the modulation
frequencies are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.284 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −57±3, corresponding to P3 = 3.52±0.01 periods
and P2 = −6.3 ± 0.3◦. The subpulses related to the trailing
component drift slightly faster than the leading component.
4.69. J1810-0100g
PSR J1810-0100g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230527 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.1061 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 79.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation (Fig. 189) show nulling and sub-
pulse drifting phenomena. The nulling fraction is estimated
from the on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 190) to be
6.1±5%. From LRFS and 2DFS of the leading proﬁle part
in Fig. 191, the positive drift feature has centroid modulation
frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.409 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 60 ± 4,
which correspond to drifting parameters P3 = 2.443±0.005
periods and P2 = 6.0 ± 0.4◦.
4.70. J1811-0154
PSR J1811-0154 was discovered by the Parkes 64-m radio
telescope (Edwards et al. 2001). Subpulse drifting was re-
ported by (Song et al. 2023) of P3 = 3.9 ± 0.2 periods and
P2 = 9+3
−2 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230405 for 64
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.9249 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 147.5 cm−3 pc from this ob-
servation. Single pulse sequences of the observation shown
in Fig. 192 illustrate the existence of subpulse drifting and
mode changing behavior. Fluctuation spectra are displayed
in Fig. 193. There is a low-frequency modulation of ∼27 pe-
riods for the on-pulse phase region. For the trailing part of
a mean pulse proﬁle, the positive drift feature widely tempo-
rally distributed has the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.235 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = 53 ± 1, which correspond to P3 = 4.26 ± 0.01
periods and P2 = 6.8 ± 0.1◦. Single pulses of two drift-
ing modes could be distinguished from the on-pulse energy

4.72. J1812+0226
PSR J1812+0226 was discovered by Stokes et al. (1985)
using the 92-m telescope at Green Bank at 390 MHz. Song
et al. (2023) has reported the drifting behaviors of two com-
ponents, and both components have two modulation fea-
tures. One component has a negative drift feature of P3 =
2.13±0.08 periods and P2 = −37+26
−22 degrees, and a positive
drifting behavior of P3 = 18 ± 9 periods and P2 = 28+19
−20
degrees. The other component has the positive drifting be-
havior of P3 = 17 ± 25 periods and P2 = 27+5
−16, as well as
a P3-only feature of P3 = 2.21 ± 0.07 periods.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201129 for 10
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.7940 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 103.8 cm−3 pc from this observa-

Figure 210. Mean polarization proﬁles (the top panel) of the weak and

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240208 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.7991 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 97.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences shown in Fig. 205 display the existence of nulling
and subpulse drifting behaviors. The nulling fraction of this
observation is estimated from the on-pulse energy histogram
in Fig. 206, which is 47±7%. From LRFS and 2DFS in
Fig. 207, the centroid modulation frequencies of the negative
drift feature are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.226 ± 0.002 and
1/P2 = −61±1, which correspond to drifting parameters of
P3 = 4.42 ± 0.04 periods and P2 = −5.9 ± 0.1◦.
4.76. J1818-0151
PSR J1818-0151 was discovered by Bates et al. (2012) in
the High Time Resolution Universe survey using the Parkes
radio telescope.
The FAST observation of this pulsar was conducted on
20210923 for 6 minutes, deriving a rotation period P =
0.8376 s and a dispersion measure DM = 200.7 cm−3 pc
from this observation.
From single pulse sequences
(Fig. 208), mode changes occur occasionally, and the central
component is greatly enhanced in the bright mode compared


## Page 85


85
to that of the normal mode (Fig. 210). The integral energy
histogram of the central phase range in the proﬁle is used to
distinguish the weak and bright emission modes, as Fig. 209
shows. In addition, the central component of the bright mode
also shows obvious modulation behavior.

41±6% has been reported previously (Navarro et al. 2003;
Rankin & Wright 2008). This pulsar also has drifting behav-
iors of about 6 and 3 periods (Rankin & Wright 2008; Song
et al. 2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250217 for 10
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0603 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 65.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 211 display nulling, subpulse drifting, as well
as the intensity weakening of the trailing component (pulses
No.250-265 and 380-390) as Rankin & Wright (2008) de-
scribed. The nulling fraction of this FAST observation is es-
timated from the on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 212, that
is 30±2%. To reduce the effect of nulls, LRFS and 2DFS


## Page 86


86
for the single pulse sequence after the removal of nulls are
obtained (Fig. 213). For the leading part in a mean pulse pro-
ﬁle, the temporal modulation frequency is widely distributed,
and the main drift feature in 2DFS is positive and with the
centroid modulation frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.348 ± 0.001
(P3 = 2.88 ± 0.01 periods) and 1/P2 = 18 ± 3 (P2 =
20 ± 3◦). For the central component, the drift feature is very
weak from this observation. In 2DFS of the trailing com-
ponent, there is a positive drift feature with the centroid of
1/P3 = 0.153 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 9 ± 2, which corre-
spond to P3 = 6.56 ± 0.04 periods and P2 = 42 ± 10◦.
P3 = 6.5 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 61 ± 54◦.

4.79. J1820+0006g
PSR J1820+0006g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240125 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.4049 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 186.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of the observation is displayed in Fig. 216. The
pulsar is found to have nulling and subpulse drifting behav-
iors. The nulling fraction is estimated to be 19±4% from the
on-pulse energy histogram (Fig. 217). For the trailing com-
ponent, 2DFS exhibits a preferred positive drift feature with
the centroid modulation frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.310±0.001
and 1/P2 = 11 ± 7, which correspond to P3 = 3.23 ± 0.01
periods and P2 = 34 ± 22◦. In addition, bright subpulses
occur in the central phase part of the proﬁle from the single
pulse sequence.

using the Molonglo radio telescope. In previous studies of
Weltevrede et al. (2007), the pulsar has a very broad feature
with a preferred drift direction of P2 = −300+50
−100 degrees
and P3 = 3 ± 1 periods at 92 cm, as well as a longitude
stationary feature of P3 = 22 ± 3 periods. Song et al. (2023)
reported the drifting parameters of P3 = 3.4 ± 0.3 periods
and P2 = −23+7
−78 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250201 for 6 min-
utes and on 20250228 for 15 minutes. From the longer ob-
servation, a rotation period P = 0.5980 s and a dispersion

4.81. J1821+0155
PSR J1821+0155 was discovered from data for the GBT
350 MHz Drift Scan Survey (Rosen et al. 2013).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210618 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.0338 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 51.8 cm−3 pc from this observation. Single
pulse sequences in Fig. 221 show subpulse drifting behavior.
From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 222, the centroid modula-
tion frequencies of the negative drift feature are estimated to
be 1/P3 = 0.2345 ± 0.0003 and 1/P2 = −25 ± 1, yield-
ing the drifting parameters of P3 = 4.26 ± 0.01 periods and
P2 = −14.2 ± 0.3◦.
4.82. J1821+4147
PSR J1821+4147 was found in the Green Bank Northern
Celestial Cap Pulsar Survey (Stovall et al. 2014). Nulling
behavior of the pulsar was identiﬁed by Lynch et al. (2018).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201031 for 8 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2619 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 41.1 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences are shown in Fig. 223. The
on-pulse integral energy histogram shown in Fig. 224 indi-
cates the existence of nulls, with the nulling fraction esti-

4.83. J1822+0044g
PSR J1822+0044g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240627 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.9989 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 55.3 cm−3 pc from this observation.
Single pulse sequences of PSR J1822+0044g in Fig. 226 il-
lustrate that there are both nulling and subpulse drifting be-
haviors. From the on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 227,
the nulling fraction is 65%. The emission duration is typ-
ically short, lasting for one to two periods. While the sin-
gle pulse segment of pulse No.627-636 shows clear sub-
pulse drifting, with P2 = −3.81 ± 0.02◦and drifting rate
D = −1.68±0.08 degrees per period, which are determined
by the cross-correlation method (Fig. 228).

PSR J1822+0705 was discovered by Foster et al. (1995)
using the Arecibo telescope. Subpulse drifting behavior was
reported by Song et al. (2023) previously.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210616 for 10
minutes, with a rotation period P = 1.3627 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 61.4 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Fig. 229 shows the clear drifting phenomenon of the
pulsar. 2DFS (Fig. 230) of the leading and trailing parts in
a mean pulse proﬁle have drift features with similar tempo-
ral modulation frequencies. For the leading part, the centroid
frequencies are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.449 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −66 ± 3, corresponding to P3 = 2.227 ± 0.004
