The single pulse sequence of this observation is shown in
Fig. 231, illustrating the existence of nulling, mode chang-
ing, as well as subpulse drifting phenomena for the ﬁrst time.
The nulling fraction is estimated from the on-pulse inte-
gral energy histogram in Fig. 232. The distribution around
the zero energy indicates the nulling behavior of this pulsar.
From ﬁtting with the Gaussian function obtained using the
off-pulse integral energy, the nulling fraction of this observa-
tion is estimated to be 25±2%.
Additionally, there seem to be 2 emission modes from the
single pulse sequence, which are labeled using red and green
colors, and deﬁned to be the bright and weak modes on the
basis of the intensity of the leading component. From aver-
aged proﬁles of two emission modes shown in Fig. 233, the
intensity ratio of the leading component to the trailing com-
ponent of the bright mode are lager than the weak mode. The
trailing part in the proﬁle of the longitude 2.5◦to 9◦of the
weak mode is stronger than that of the bright mode.

## Page 93


93
Weak and bright emission modes both have subpulse drift-
ing behavior. To obtain the drifting parameters, ﬂuctuation
spectra of two components for the weak emission mode and
the leading component for the bright emission mode are dis-
played in Fig. 234. For the weak emission mode, drift bands
are clear in the single pulse sequence (Fig. 231). There are
positive drift features in 2DFS of the leading and trailing pro-
ﬁle parts. For the weak emission mode, the centroid frequen-
cies of the drift feature in 2DFS are 1/P3 = 0.298 ± 0.004
(P3 = 3.36 ± 0.05 periods) and 1/P2 = 57 ± 10 (P2 =
6 ± 2◦) for the leading part, and 1/P3 = 0.301 ± 0.003
(P3 = 3.32±0.03 periods) and 1/P2 = 68±12 (P2 = 6±1◦)
for the trailing part of a mean pulse proﬁle. For the bright
emission mode, 2DFS of the leading part of the proﬁle ex-
hibits a negative drift feature, with the centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.452 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = −52 ± 8, correspond-
ing to P3 = 2.21 ± 0.02 periods and P2 = −7 ± 1◦.
More observations are needed for a more accurate analysis
of this pulsar.

showed this pulsar exhibiting dwarf pulses during the nulling

state. Song et al. (2023) reported negative drifting param-
eters of two components, they are P3 = 3.5 ± 0.1 and
P2 = −49+40
−2 degrees for the leading component, and P3 =
3.3 ± 0.1 and P2 = −44+25
−39 degrees for the trailing compo-
nent. There is also a P3-only feature of 26 periods.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211223 for 40
minutes and on 20220902 for 15 minutes.
From the 40-
minute data, a rotation period P = 2.4995 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 63.9 cm−3 pc were determined. Here
we show the result of the observation on 20211223. The
nulling fraction is estimated to be 34±3% from the on-pulse
integral energy histogram in Fig. 237.
Fluctuation spec-
tra of this observation are shown in Fig. 238, from which
the negative drifting parameters are estimated.
For lead-
ing part of a mean pulse proﬁle, the drift feature in 2DFS
has centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.309 ± 0.003 and
1/P2 = −23 ± 6, corresponding to P3 = 3.24 ± 0.03 peri-
ods and P2 = −16±4◦. For the trailing part, the frequencies
are 1/P3 = 0.302 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −27 ± 7, yielding
P3 = 3.31 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = −14 ± 3◦. The P3-only
feature reported by Song et al. (2023) is probably caused by
the nulling behavior.
4.87. J1825+0004
PSR J1825+0004 was discovered by Dewey et al. (1985)
using the 92 m telescope at Green Bank at 390 MHz. P3-
only feature of 14.1±0.9 periods was reported by Song et al.
(2023).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210826 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.7790 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 56.3 cm−3 pc from this observation.
Single pulse sequences are shown in Fig. 239, displaying the
subpulse drifting phenomenon. For the leading part in a mean
pulse proﬁle, 2DFS in Fig. 241 exhibit a negative drift fea-
ture with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.169 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −77±1, which correspond to P3 = 5.90±0.04
periods and P2 = −4.6 ± 0.1◦. For the weak trailing part
of the proﬁle, there is also a temporal modulation feature in
2DFS, with the centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.151 ± 0.003
(P3 = 6.6 ± 0.1 periods). Additionally, on-pulse integral en-
ergies of two single pulses (Nos. 627 and 628) are less than
3σE.

4.88. J1825-0208g
PSR J1825-0208g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20221109 for 16
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 3.3076 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 192.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 242 display nulling and subpulse drift-
ing phenomena.
The on-pulse integral energy histogram
(Fig. 243) indicates the nulling fraction to be 48±8%. From
the correlation between adjacent single pulses in Fig. 244,
the pulsar has negative subpulse drifting with a drift rate of
D = −0.84 ± 0.04 degrees per period.

4.91. J1827-0125g
PSR J1827-0125g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220329 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.3379 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 97.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are displayed in Fig. 250, where the central part
in the proﬁle has subpulse drifting behavior.
Fluctuation
spectra in Fig. 251 illustrate that the central and trailing
proﬁle parts have different modulation properties. For the
central part in a mean pulse proﬁle, 2DFS exhibits a pre-
ferred negative drift feature with the centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.105 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −2.1 ± 0.3, correspond-
ing to P3 = 9.5 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −175 ± 27◦. The
centroid temporal modulation frequency in 2DFS of the trail-
ing part is 1/P3 = 0.375±0.003, yielding P3 = 2.66±0.02
periods.

4.93. J1830-1135
PSR J1830-1135 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam
Pulsar Survey (Morris et al. 2002). A drift feature in 2DFS
with P2 = 25+25
−7 degrees and P3 = 2.1 ± 0.2 periods was
reported by Weltevrede et al. (2007) at 21 cm.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20260419 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 6.2210 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 250.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 255. From the ﬂuctuation spec-
tra in Fig. 256, the drift feature in 2DFS has the centroid at
1/P3 = 0.391±0.002 and 1/P2 = 105±2, corresponding to
periodicities of P3 = 2.56±0.02 periods and P2 = 3.4±0.1
degrees.

4.96. J1832+0029
PSR J1832+0029 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Lorimer et al. 2006). The pulsar is known as
an intermittent pulsar, with off-state time scale fraction of
about 50% reported by Lorimer et al. (2012).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20191009 for 5 min-
utes and 20210226 for 10 minutes, and on on-state during
these two observations. Single pulse sequences of the ob-
servation on 20210226 are shown in Fig. 262. 2DFS of the
leading and trailing parts of the proﬁle in Fig. 263 illustrate
the existence of the low-frequency modulation, with the cen-
troid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.055 ± 0.002 (P3 = 18 ± 1

4.97. J1832+0203g
PSR J1832+0203g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240422 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2127 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 226.2 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of this observation is displayed in Fig. 264. The
LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 265 indicate that the leading and
trailing parts in a mean pulse proﬁle have similar temporal
modulation frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.103 ± 0.001, corre-
sponding to P3 = 9.7 ± 0.1 periods. 2DFS of the trailing
part exhibits a preferred positive subpulse drift feature, with
the centroid of 1/P2 = 5 ± 2, yielding P2 = 73 ± 34◦.
4.98. J1832-1142g
PSR J1832-1142g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20251201 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0308 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 704.0 cm−3 pc. The observation in-
dicates that this pulsar has weak and strong emission modes.
The single pulse sequence in Fig. 266 displays three seg-
ments of enhanced emission. Single pulses of two emission

4.101. J1835+00
PSR J1835+00 was discovered in the Pulsar Arecibo L-
band Feed Array (PALFA) survey (Parent et al. 2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220614 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.7900 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 134.6 cm−3 pc determined. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 274 display the unsystematic subpulse drift-
ing phenomenon. Fluctuation spectra are shown in Fig. 275,
and both negative and positive drift features are present in
the 2DFS. The centroid of the negative drift feature is at
1/P3 = 0.060 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −13 ± 2, corresponding
to periodicities of P3 = 16.7±0.5 periods and P2 = −28±4
degrees. For the positive drift feature, the centroid frequen-
cies are 1/P3 = 0.164 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 18 ± 2, yielding
P3 = 6.1 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 20 ± 3 degrees.
4.102. J1835+0301g
PSR J1835+0301g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240418 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.2987 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 206.6 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. The single pulse sequence in Fig. 276 shows the nulling

integrated energy histogram (Fig. 277).

4.104. J1835-0149g
PSR J1835-0149g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220407 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2775 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 104.2 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences and ﬂuctuation spectra of the
observation are displayed in Fig. 280 and 281. The pulsar
has subpulse drifting and low-frequency modulation behav-
iors. Leading and trailing longitude parts both have a P3 of
6±1 periods, with P2 of 3.1±0.9 and 9±6 degrees. Addition-
ally, there are also low-frequency modulations for two parts,
with periods of 89±36 and 50±17 periods.

periods), 1/P2 = 34 ± 1 (P2 = 10.7 ± 0.2 degrees). The
drifting properties derived from the observation on 20240329
are consistent with those obtained on 20240115.
4.107. J1837+0053
PSR J1837+0053 was found in the Parkes 20-cm multi-
beam pulsar survey of the Galactic plane (Lorimer et al.
2006).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20191009 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.4735 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 123.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences are shown in Fig. 286, illustrating the existence
of nulls. From the on-pulse integrated energy histogram in
Fig. 287, the nulling fraction of this observation is estimated
to be 61±4%. There are also some weak emissions such as
pulses around Nos. 40 and 320, which result in distributions
of nulling and emission not well separated in the histogram.

4.111. J1838-0014g
PSR J1838-0014g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).

vation. Single pulse sequences and ﬂuctuation spectra are

periods), without any preferred phase modulation.

ﬂat featureless spectrum of the central part, and a temporally
wide modulation feature of the trailing part. In 2DFS of the
leading proﬁle part, the centroid of the positive drift feature

4.114. J1839-1238
PSR J1839-1238 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Lorimer et al. 2006). Song et al. (2023) re-
ported a drifting behavior with P3 = 34 ± 6 periods and
P2 = −92+69
−78 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250422 for 11
minutes, driving a rotation period P = 1.9113 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 174.2 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 302 illustrate the existence of nulling and
subpulse drifting phenomena. The nulling fraction is esti-
mated to be 21.1±1.6% from the on-pulse energy histogram
of single pulses (Fig. 303), and nulling gives rise to the
lowfrequency modulation feature in the ﬂuctuation spectra
in Fig. 304. In addition, the negative drift feature in 2DFS
exhibits the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.172 ± 0.003
and 1/P2 = −91 ± 3, corresponding to periodicities of
P3 = 5.8 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −3.9 ± 0.1 degrees.
4.115. J1840+0151g
PSR J1840+0151g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210513 and
20250620 for 15 and 5 minutes, respectively. From the data

PSR J1840-0141g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220118 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.9269 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 278.2 cm−3 pc from this observation.
Single pulse sequences in Fig. 307 indicate the existence of
nulling of subpulse drifting behaviors. The nulling fraction
of this observation is estimated to be 44±5% from the on-
pulse energy histogram in Fig. 308. The drifting parameters
are obtained from the intensity correlation of adjacent sin-
gle pulses of Nos. 69-82 and 102-108 (Fig. 309), that are
D = 0.7 ± 0.2 degrees per period and P2 = 2.13 ± 0.05◦.
4.117. J1840-0626
PSR J1840-0626 was discovered by Keith et al. (2009) in
the data from the Parkes Multi-beam Pulsar Survey.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20251015 for 15
minutes, and a rotation period P = 1.8935 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 760.5 cm−3 pc were determined. The
single pulse sequence in Fig. 310 shows mode changes be-
tween the weak and bright emission modes. Single pulses
of two emission modes are distinguished from the 3-period
smoothed energy variation histogram (Fig. 311). In the ﬁg-
ures, weak and bright modes are labeled with red and green
colors, respectively.

−3

−76 degrees.

in Fig. 312 reveal a positive drift with P3 ∼2 periods, as well

4.119. J1841+0912
PSR J1841+0912 was discovered by Manchester et al.
(1978) using observations at the Molonglo Radio Observa-
tory and the Australian National Radio Astronomy Obser-
vatory, Parkes. From previous studies, the nulling fraction
was estimated to be no more than 5% at 430 MHz (Weis-
berg et al. 1986) and less than 2% at 327 MHz (Herﬁndal
& Rankin 2009). This pulsar also has modulation phenom-
ena (Weltevrede et al. 2007; Herﬁndal & Rankin 2009). Song
et al. (2023) reported the drifting parameters of P3 = 51±39
periods and P2 = 68+37
−44 degrees.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230206 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.3813 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 49.2 cm−3 pc. The nulling fraction
of this observation is about 1.0±0.1% from the on-pulse en-
ergy histogram in Fig. 316, with the duration of nulls being
no more than 2 periods (Fig. 314). In ﬂuctuation spectra,
there is a low-frequency drift feature with the centroid fre-
quencies of 1/P3 = 0.020 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 4 ± 1, which
correspond to P3 = 49 ± 1 periods and P2 = 100 ± 31◦.

4.121. J1841-0310
PSR J1841-0310 was ﬁrst reported by Hobbs et al. (2004)
from the Parkes multibeam pulsar survey.
Burke-Spolaor
et al. (2012) revealed that the pulsar appears to be nulling
on 1352MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20231026 for 15
minutes, 20240327 for 2 minutes and 20250721 for 5 min-
utes.
From the 15-minute data, a rotation period P
=
1.6578 s and a dispersion measure DM = 223.8 cm−3 pc
were derived. Single pulse sequences of the observation on
20231026 are displayed in Fig. 320, illustrating that the pul-
sar has the nulling phenomenon and the emission could last
for just one period. The nulling fraction of this observation is
estimated to be 65.2±6.0% from the on-pulse integral energy
histogram (Fig. 321).
4.122. J1841-0345
PSR J1841-0345 was discovered by Lorimer et al. (2000)
with the Effelsberg 100-m radio telescope. Negative drifting
periodicities of P3 = 27 ± 18 periods and P2 = −258+160
−134
degrees were reported by Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250304 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.2041 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 196.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 200-600 in
Fig. 322 show an unsystematic negative drifting behavior.
Fluctuation spectra in Fig. 323 display that the negative drift
feature is widely distributed in 1/P3, consistent with the un-
systematic drifting. The centroid of the feature in 2DFS is
at 1/P3 = 0.0528 ± 0.0003 and 1/P2 = −3.7 ± 0.3, cor-
responding to P3 = 18.9 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −99 ± 8
degrees.
4.123. J1842+0131g
PSR J1842+0131g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220720 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.5903 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 115.8 cm−3 pc from this observa-
tion. Single pulse sequences in Fig. 324 and ﬂuctuation spec-
tra in Fig. 325 illustrate that this pulsar has a temporal mod-
ulation feature with a frequency of 1/P3 = 0.461 ± 0.003,
corresponding to P3 = 2.17 ± 0.01 periods.
4.124. J1842-0153
PSR J1842-0153 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam
Pulsar Survey (Morris et al. 2002). P3-only feature of P3 =
28 ± 10 periods has been reported by (Song et al. 2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230328 for 70
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0542 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 426.5 cm−3 pc. Segments of single
pulses are shown in Fig. 4.120, which reveal two phenomena:
nulling and subpulse drifting.

This pulsar also has nulling behavior. The nulling frac-
tion is estimated to be 6.1% from the intensity histogram in
Fig. 330. From the distribution of continuous period num-
bers for adjacent nulling and emission states in Fig. 331, the
duration of the nulling state is short, ranging from 1 to 7 peri-
ods. The duration of the emission state peaks at a short value,
while a signiﬁcant portion of the values cluster at a longer
duration. Single-pulse starting longitudes are smoothed and
interpolated at nulls, and the histogram of longitudes related
to nulling and emission states in Fig. 332 indicates that nulls
tend to appear at later longitudes, reﬂecting the relation be-
tween nulling and drifting direction changes.

PSR J1843-0127g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220202 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.1648 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 449.0 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence in Figures 343 shows intensity variation. From the
on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 344, there is evidence that
the pulsar has nulling behavior, with a nulling fraction esti-
mated to be 24(2)% for this observation.
4.130. J1843-0137
PSR J1843-0137 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230508 for 36
minutes, with a rotation period P = 0.6698 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 484.7 cm−3 pc determined. The single
pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 1000-
1500 in Fig. 345 show the modulation phenomenon. In the


## Page 127


127
ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 346, the centroid frequency of the
modulation feature is 1/P3 = 0.0453 ± 0.0004, correspond-
ing to the periodicity of P3 = 22.1 ± 0.2 periods.
4.131. J1843-0521
PSR J1930+2836 was discovered by FAST in the
Commensal Radio Astronomy FAST Survey (CRAFTS)
(http://groups.bao.ac.cn/ism/CRAFTS/).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250821 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.4705 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 771.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 347 show the nulling phenomenon. From
the on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 348), the nulling
fraction of this observation is estimated to be 47.1±3.4%.
4.132. J1843-0757
PSR J1843-0757 was discovered by MeerTRAP with the
MeerKAT telescope (Bezuidenhout et al. 2022). A nulling
fraction of around 35 percent was reported by Sengar et al.
(2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20251015 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.0321 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 254.2 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 349 show nulling and subpulse drifting
phenomena. The nulling fraction of this observation is es-
timated to be 54.1±5.3% from the on-pulse integral energy
histogram (Fig. 350). Fluctuation spectra are displayed in
Fig. 351. For 2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part, the centroid
frequencies of the positive drift feature are estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.403 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 25 ± 6, corresponding to
periodicities of P3 = 2.48 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = 15 ± 4
degrees.
4.133. J1844+00
PSR J1844+00 was discovered using the Arecibo radio
telescope at 430 MHz (Camilo et al. 1996a). The nulling
phenomenon of this pulsar was ﬁrst reported by Wang et al.
(2020) using the Jiamusi 66 m telescope at a central fre-
quency of 2250 MHz. The pulsar was observed for 180 min-
utes, and 256 nulling pulses were detected by integrating 32
pulses to form a subintegration. The nulling fraction was es-
timated to be between 0.3% and 34.0%.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210713,
20211008, and 20211222, with each observation conducted
for 15 minutes.
From the data of 20211222, a rotation
period P = 0.4605 s and a dispersion measure DM =
345.3 cm−3 pc were determined. Single pulse sequences of
the observation on 20211008 and 20211222 are shown in
Fig. 352, and 2DFS of the leading and trailing parts in the
proﬁles are displayed in Fig. 353. For the leading part in the
proﬁle, there are two modulation features in 2DFS with the
centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.068 ± 0.003 (P3 = 15 ± 1
periods) and 1/P3 = 0.422 ± 0.003 (P3 = 2.37 ± 0.02
periods) from the observation on 20211008.
The tempo-
ral modulation feature in 2DFS of the trailing part is more
widely distributed, with the centroid frequency of 1/P3 =

4.135. J1844-0433
PSR J1844-0433 was discovered by the MKIA telescope
(Clifton et al. 1987). Weltevrede et al. (2006) reported the
negative drift behavior at 21 cm with P3 = 8.5 ± 0.1 periods
and P2 = −13+11
5
degrees. Song et al. (2023) presented the
drift periodicities of P3 = 8.1 ± 0.3 periods and P2 = −5+2
−4
degrees measured at 1280 MHz with the MeerKAT telescope.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210219 for 2 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.9910 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 124.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence in Fig. 356 displays negative drifting for the lead-
ing part in the mean pulse proﬁle and nulling. The nulling
fraction is estimated to be 12±1% from the on-pulse en-
ergy histogram of single pulses (Fig. 357). From LRFS and
2DFS of the leading proﬁle part in Fig. 358, the centroid of
the negative drift feature is at 1/P3 = 0.112 ± 0.002 and
1/P2 = −66 ± 13, corresponding to P3 = 8.9 ± 0.1 periods
and P2 = −5 ± 1 degrees.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250616 and
20250829, both for 15 minutes. From the data of 20250616,
a rotation period of P = 1.0822 s and a dispersion measure
of DM = 913.0 cm−3 pc were derived. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 550-800 in
Fig. 364 display a substantial fraction of nulls. The nulling
fraction is estimated from the on-pulse integral energy his-
togram (Fig. 365), which is 81.3±5.5% for this observation.
4.139. J1845-0743
PSR J1845-0743 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam
Pulsar Survey (Kramer et al. 2003).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250321 for 6 min-
utes, driving a rotation period of P = 0.1047 s and a disper-
sion measure of DM = 281.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 3000-3200 are
shown in Fig. 366. From LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 367, this
pulsar exhibits the modulation behavior. The modulation fea-
ture has a centroid at 1/P3 = 0.095 ± 0.002, corresponding
to P3 = 10.6 ± 0.2 periods.
4.140. J1846+0657g
PSR J1846+0657g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240905 for 15
minutes, driving a rotation period of P = 2.2442 s and a
dispersion measure of DM = 223.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences and the on-pulse integral energy histogram of this
observation are shown in Fig. 368 and 369, illustrating the
existence of the nulling phenomenon. The nulling fraction of
this data is estimated to be 28.7±2.8% from Fig. 369.

4.142. J1846-07492
PSR J1846-07492 was discovered in data from the Parkes
Multi-beam Pulsar Survey (Keith et al. 2009). Song et al.
(2023) reported two drift features: one with P3 = 2.7 ± 0.4
periods and P2 = −98+89
−35 degrees, and the other with P3 =
21 ± 9 periods and P2 = −83+48
−53 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250321 for 6 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.8613 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 191.1 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 372 display the negative drifting behavior and a low-
frequency modulation superimposed on it. Fluctuation spec-
tra are shown in Fig. 373. The negative drift feature in 2DFS
is widely spread in 1/P3, with the centroid frequencies of

4.143. J1847+0614g
PSR J1847+0614g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210220 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.6631 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 273.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation displayed in Fig. 374 show the
existence of a negative subpulse drifting behavior. In ﬂuctu-
ation spectra (Fig. 384), the centroid frequencies of the main
drift feature are 1/P3 = 0.327±0.001 and 1/P2 = −49±1,
which correspond to P3 = 3.06 ± 0.01 periods and P2 =
−7.4 ± 0.2◦.
4.144. J1847-0048g
PSR J1847-0048g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211113 for 15
minutes and 20220606 for 30 minutes.
From the data of
20211113, a rotation period P = 0.5825 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 664.6 cm−3 pc were determined. Single
pulse sequences of the observation on 20211113 are shown in
Fig. 376, which display the modulation behavior. In the ﬂuc-
tuation spectra (Fig. 377), the centroid frequency of the low-
frequency feature is estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.056 ± 0.001,
corresponding to P3 = 17.9 ± 0.4 periods.
The temporal modulation periodicity is 16±2 periods. Sin-
gle pulse behavior of the observation on 20220606 is consis-
tent with 20211113.

4.145. J1847-0402
PSR J1847-0402 was discovered by Davies et al. (1970)
using the Mark I radio telescope at Jodrell Bank. Weltevrede
et al. (2006) reported the drifting behavior at 21 cm with
P3 = 12 ± 1 periods and P2 = 80+70
−45 degrees. At 1280
MHz, Song et al. (2023) presented two modulation features
for two components: a P3-only feature with P3 = 71±39 pe-
riods for one component, and a drift feature with P3 = 12±2
periods and P2 = 57+15
−25 degrees for the other.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20221227 for 9 min-
utes and on 20250321 for 6 minutes. From the longer data,
a rotation period P = 0.5978 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 141.5 cm−3 pc were determined. Single pulse se-
quences of the observation on 20221227 in Fig. 378 show
the subpulse modulation phenomenon. Fluctuation spectra
are shown in Fig. 379.
For the leading phase part in the
mean pulse proﬁle, the main modulation feature is at low fre-
quency, with a centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.024 ± 0.001,
corresponding to P3 = 42±2 periods. In the 2DFS, the trail-
ing proﬁle part also exhibits this low-frequency feature, with
a centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.026 ± 0.001 (P3 = 39 ± 2
periods), as well as an additional feature with the centroid at
1/P3 = 0.080 ± 0.001 (P3 = 12.4 ± 0.2 periods). The mod-
ulation properties observed on 20250321 are consistent with
those observed on 20221227.

4.146. J1848+0127g
PSR J1848+0127g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230306 for 17
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.5339 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 77.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation are shown in Fig. 380, where the
leading component displays weak and bright emission states.

pulse proﬁle in Fig. 381, we distinguish the weak and bright
modes for single pulses. The integral polarization proﬁles
and PA curves of two modes are shown in Fig. 382.

PSR J1849+0225g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230309 for 37
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.4744 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 266.4 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation are shown in Fig. 389. In the
energy histogram in Fig. 390, the distribution around zero in-
tensity is not symmetric, which illustrates that the pulsar has
a weak emission state instead of the nulling state. Emission
modes of single pulses are distinguished from the histogram.
Averaged proﬁles of two emission modes in Fig. 391 show
that the peak of the weak mode is earlier compared to the
bright mode.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230309 for 41
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.6666 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 350.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences of this observation are shown in Fig. 392. The
on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 393 indicates the exis-
tence of the nulling phenomenon with a nulling fraction of
16±2%. From the ﬂuctuation spectra of the on-pulse phase
region (Fig. 393), there is a temporal modulation with the
centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.185 ± 0.001, corresponding
to P3 = 5.40 ± 0.03 periods.

PSR J1850+0026 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam

ported parameters of three negative drift features: one from
the leading component of P3 = 9 ± 2 periods and P2 =
−65+31
−18 degree; two from the trailing component, that are

−28 degrees, and
P3 = 4.1 ± 0.2 periods and P2 = −10+2
−4 degrees.

spectra are shown in Fig. 396. There is a negative drift fea-
ture in 2DFS of the leading component, with the centroid
frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.081 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −6 ± 1,

4.158. J1851-0029
PSR J1851-0029 was discovered by Keith et al. (2009) us-
ing data of the Parkes Multi-beam Pulsar Survey. P3-only
feature with the modulation periodicity of 10.0±0.5 has been
reported by Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220121 for 15
minutes and 20230508 for 34 minutes.
From the longer
data, a rotation period and a dispersion measure are de-
rived to be P = 0.5187 s and a dispersion measure DM =
521.5 cm−3 pc. The single pulse sequence and a zoomed-in
view of the observation on 20230508 are shown in Fig. 408,
displaying the modulation behavior.
From the ﬂuctuation
spectra in Fig. 409, the centroid modulation frequency is
estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.087 ± 0.001, corresponding to
P3 = 11.5 ± 0.1 periods.
4.159. J1851-0053
PSR J1851-0053 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004). (Song et al. 2023) re-
ported the drifting behavior of P3 = 3.5(3) periods and
P2 = 3.70+13
−0.08 degrees.
The pulsar was observed by FAST for 5 minutes on
20210822 and for 15 minutes each on 20210903, 20211009,
and 20250331. From the observation on 20210822, a rotation
period and a dispersion measure are determined to be P =

Figure 428. Cross correlation of PSR J1853+0853 from the FAST obser-
vation on 20210306.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230528 for 1.7
hours and 20251019 for 15 minutes. From the observation
with a longer duration, the rotation period and a dispersion
measure are derived to be P = 3.0195 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 236.5 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences of
the observation on 20251019 are shown in Fig. 419, illustrat-
ing the existence of nulling and subpulse drifting behaviors.
The emission could last for only one period, such as pulse
Nos. 215 and 224. From the on-pulse integral energy his-
togram, the nulling fraction of this data is estimated to be
54.2±3.0%. LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 421 display two mod-
ulation features. The low-frequency modulation feature with
the centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.046±0.001 is caused by
the nulling behavior, yielding P3 = 21.7± periods. The neg-
ative drift feature is characterized by the centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.435 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = −89 ± 3, which cor-
respond to the modulation frequencies of P3 = 2.30 ± 0.02
periods and P2 = −4.0 ± 0.2 degrees.
4.164. J1853+0029
PSR J1853+0029 was discovered in the Pulsar Arecibo L-
band Feed Array (PALFA) survey (Parent et al. 2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200920 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.8769 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 227.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 422 display nulling and mode changing
with the emission of the trailing proﬁle part occasionally en-
hanced. The nulling fraction of this observation is estimated
to be 4±1% from the on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 423.
Single pulses of two emission modes are distinguished from
the energy histogram (Fig. 424), where each energy value
is obtained by integrating over the trailing proﬁle part and
then smoothing the resulting time series over 7 periods. The
trailing-weak and trailing-strong modes are labeled in red
and green, respectively. The mean proﬁles of the two emis-
sion modes (Fig. 425) both exhibit a stronger trailing part,
while the contrast between trailing and leading is much more
pronounced in the trailing-strong mode.

sar survey (Lorimer et al. 2006).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210306 and

4.166. J1854-0230g
PSR J1854-0230g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230527 for 14
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.6874 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 548.6 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of this observation (Fig. 429) shows that there are
weak and bright emission modes. The polarization proﬁles of
two emission modes are displayed in Fig. 430, where the pro-
ﬁle shape changes a lot. In the bright mode, the central com-
ponent is the strongest. While in the weak emission mode,
the intensity of the leading component is the highest. Ad-
ditionally, the bright emission mode displays subpulse drift-
ing behavior. From ﬂuctuation spectra of the leading compo-
nent (Fig. 431), the centroid frequencies of the drift feature

4.167. J1854+0319
PSR J1854+0319 was discovered in the PALFA survey
Lyne et al. (2017). The drifting behavior of the trailing com-
ponent was published by Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200509 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.6285 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 481.0 cm−3 pc. From this observation,
we newly ﬁnd that the pulsar exhibits changes between dif-
ferent drifting states, and the weaker leading component also
has drifting behavior. Single pulse sequences are displayed
in Fig. 432, with two drifting modes labeled using bars of dif-
ferent colors. Drifting parameters of two drifting modes are
obtained from ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 433. For the slow
drifting mode, the centroid frequencies of the drift feature in
2DFS are 1/P3 = 0.085 ± 0.001 (P3 = 11.8 ± 0.2 periods)
and 1/P2 = −28 ± 3 (P2 = −13 ± 1◦) for the leading part
in the mean pulse proﬁle, and 1/P3 = 0.086 ± 0.001 (P3 =
11.7 ± 0.1 periods) and 1/P2 = −13 ± 1 (P2 = −27 ± 3◦)
for the trailing part. While for the fast drifting mode, they
are 1/P3 = 0.097 ± 0.002 (P3 = 10.3 ± 0.2 periods) and

4.172. J1857+0210
PSR J1857+0210 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam
Pulsar Survey (Morris et al. 2002).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220925 for 10
minutes, and a rotation period P = 0.6310 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 782.8 cm−3 pc were derived.
The
single pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No.
400-600 in Fig. 446 show the intensity modulation behav-
ior. LRFS and 2DFS are displayed in Fig. 447, and the cen-
troid frequency of the modulation feature is estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.125 ± 0.002, corresponding to the periodicity of
P3 = 8.0 ± 0.1 periods.
4.173. J1857+0943
PSR J1857+0943 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Segelstein et al. 1986).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210425 for 5
minutes and on 20221001 for 26 minutes, with a rotation
period P = 0.0054 s and a dispersion measure DM =
13.3 cm−3 pc derived. Zoomed-in views of 400 pulses for
the main pulse and interpulse from two observations are
shown in Fig. 448. From the LRFS of the main pulse and
interpulse in Fig. 449, the temporal modulation frequency is
widely distributed, and the main modulation properties are
different for the main pulse and interpulse. For the data on
20210425, the centroid frequency of the main modulation
feature is 1/P3 = 0.1951 ± 0.0005 (P3 = 5.13 ± 0.01 peri-
ods) for the main pulse, and 0.093 ± 0.001 (P3 = 10.8 ± 0.1
periods) for the interpulse. For the data on 20221001, the
derived parameters are 1/P3 = 0.1934 ± 0.0003 (P3 =
5.17±0.01 periods) for the main pulse, and 1/P3 = 0.0926±
0.0003 (P3 = 10.80 ± 0.03 periods) for the interpulse.

data, a rotation period P = 1.2225 s and a dispersion mea-
sure DM = 352.6 cm−3 pc were determined. The single
pulse sequence of the observation on 20220429 is displayed
in Fig. 450. From LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 451, the centroid

4.176. J1858+0241
PSR J1858+0241 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200806 and
20220529, both for 15 minutes. The rotation period and a
dispersion measure were determined to be P = 4.6929 s and
DM = 327.2 cm−3 pc from the observation on 20200806.
Single pulse sequences shown in Fig. 455 illustrate the exis-
tence of the nulling phenomenon. From the on-pulse integral
energy histograms (Fig. 456), the nulling fractions of two ob-
servations are estimated to be 47±4% and 46±3%, respec-
tively. The subpulse drifting behavior appears to be unstable
in the single-pulse sequences. However, more observations
of longer duration are required for a detailed analysis.
4.177. J1858+0319
PSR J1858+0319 was discovered with the Arecibo Tele-
scope (Lyne et al. 2017).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201026 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.8675 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 282.9 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence of the observation is shown in Fig. 457, which illus-
trates the drifting bands temporally modulated at a low fre-
quency. From the LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 458, the centroid
frequencies of the drift feature are 1/P3 = 0.024 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −26 ± 6, corresponding to periodicities of the
subpulse drifting P3 = 42 ± 1 periods and P2 = −14 ± 3◦.
4.178. J1858+0609g
PSR J1858+0609g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210822 and
20260103 for 5 and 13 minutes, respectively.
From the
longer data, a rotation period P = 0.5596 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 422.3 cm−3 pc were derived.
The
single pulse sequence observed on 20260103, along with
the zoomed-in view of pulses No. 1000-1400 presented in
Fig. 459, indicates the existence of subpulse drifting. Fluctu-
ation spectra are shown in Fig. 460. The centroid of the neg-
ative drift feature is at 1/P3 = 0.041 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 =
−18 ± 1, corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 24 ± 1 pe-
riods and P2 = −20 ± 1 degrees. drift features observed on
20210822 are consistent with those on 20260103.
4.179. J1859+00
PSR J1859+00 was discovered by Camilo et al. (1996a)
using the Arecibo radio telescope at 430 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230308 for 30
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.5596 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 422.3 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 461. There are three modulation

features in 2DFS. One feature has a low temporal frequency
without phase modulation, while the other two features are
phase modulated in opposite directions. The centroid fre-
quency of the low-frequency modulation feature in 2DFS is
1/P3 = 0.160 ± 0.002, corresponding to P3 = 6.2 ± 0.1
periods. The temporal modulation frequencies of the two
oppositely drift features are widely distributed in 2DFS.
Meanwhile, the phase modulation frequencies of these fea-
tures are opposite in value, with the negative drifting be-
ing more dominant. The negative drift feature is character-
ized by the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.356 ± 0.002
and 1/P2 = −18.4 ± 0.1, yielding P3 = 2.81 ± 0.01 pe-
riods and P2 = −19.6 ± 0.1◦. For the positive drift fea-
ture, the centroid frequencies are 1/P3 = 0.404 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = 18.1 ± 0.1, which correspond to P3 = 2.48 ± 0.01
periods and P2 = 19.8 ± 0.1◦. These indicate that widths
between subpulses are constant, but the drifting rate varies
with time. Oppositely drift feature in 2DFS may be caused
by variable drifting rate and the aliased effect.

minutes and 20201223 for 30 minutes. From the 30-minute
data, a rotation period P = 0.3743 s and a dispersion mea-

4.183. J1901+0124
PSR J1901+0124 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004). Song et al. (2023) reported
that the pulsar has the negative drifting behavior with P3 =
10 ± 10 periods and P2 = −77+38
−163 degrees.
This pulsar is observed by FAST on 20201218 for 5 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.3188 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 314.4 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 471 show the modulation phenomenon. In the ﬂuc-
tuation spectra in Fig. 472, the temporal modulation fre-
quency is wide. The centroid of the modulation feature is
at 1/P3 = 0.095 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −1.8 ± 0.7, corre-
sponding to periodicities of P3 = 10.5 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = −201 ± 78 degrees.
4.184. J1901+0156
PSR J1901+0156 was discovered by the 92-m telescope at
Green Bank (Stokes et al. 1985). Periodic modulation behav-
ior of P3 = 14 ± 1 periods was detected by Weltevrede et al.
(2007); Basu et al. (2020b). Song et al. (2023) reported the
negative drift feature for two components: P3 = 14 ± 3 peri-

drifting with a 2.2-period temporal modulation periodicity
for two components was reported by Song et al. (2023).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220102 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 4.6008 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 406.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences are shown in Fig. 477. The pulsar has nulling
and subpulse drifting behaviors. The nulling fraction of this
FAST observation is estimated to be 24±3% from the on-
pulse integral energy histogram in Fig. 478. 2DFS of the
trailing component (Fig. 479) shows a positive drift feature,
with centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.457 ± 0.002 and
1/P2 = 88 ± 12, corresponding to drifting periodicities of
P3 = 2.19 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = 4.1 ± 0.6◦.
4.187. J1901-0104g
PSR J1901-0104g was found in the FAST GPPS survey
(Han et al. 2021, 2025).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220420 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.7394 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 261.4 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 480. The ﬂuctuation spectra in
Fig. 481 exhibit a temporal modulation feature with the cen-
troid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.061±0.002, which corresponds
to P3 = 16.3 ± 0.4 periods.
4.188. J1902+0248
PSR J1902+0248 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004).
The pulsar is observed by FAST on 20210119 and
20221018 for both 5 minutes.
Single pulse sequences in
Fig. 482 display that the pulsar has nulling phenomenon,
and the trailing component shows clear modulation. Nulling
fractions of two observations are estimated to be 16±2%

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220410 for 15
minutes, and a rotation period of P = 0.4878 s and a dis-
persion measure of DM = 105.5 cm−3 pc were determined.
Single pulse sequences in Fig. 485 show the subpulse drift-
ing phenomenon. LRFS and 2DFS are displayed in Fig. 486.
The main feature in 2DFS is related to the positively drifting
behavior, which is characterized by the centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.081±0.001 and 1/P2 = 8±1, corresponding to
periodicities of 1/P3 = 12.4±0.2 periods and 1/P2 = 46±6
degrees.


## Page 170


170
4.190. J1902+1022g
PSR J1902+1022g was discovered by the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
The pulsar was observed by the FAST on 20220905 for
15 minutes, deriving a rotation period of P = 0.5019 s
and a dispersion measure of DM = 288.8 cm−3 pc. The
single pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view of this obser-
vation in Fig. 487 illustrate the changes between the weak
and bright emission modes. The on-pulse integral energy se-
quence of single pulses is smoothed using a 5-period moving
average. Single pulses of two modes are distinguished from
the smoothed on-pulse energy histogram in Figure 488. The
weak and bright emission modes are labeled using red and
green, respectively.
4.191. J1902+1141
PSR J1902+1141 was discovered in the PALFA survey by
(Patel et al. 2018), and the long-term timing results were
lately presented by Parent et al. (2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230825 for 15
minutes, with a rotation period P = 0.4092 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 269.1 cm−3 pc derived. The single
pulse sequence of this observation and a zoomed-in view of
pulses No. 1000-1400 are shown in Figure 489, displaying
a negative drifting phenomenon, as well as a low-frequency
modulation behavior. From LRFS and 2DFS in Figure 490,
the negative drift feature, with temporally modulated fre-
quency widely distributed from 0.2 to 0.5, has centroid fre-
quencies of 1/P3 = 0.345 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = −48 ± 1,
corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 2.90 ± 0.02 peri-
ods and P2 = −7.4 ± 0.1 degrees.
The low-frequency
modulation feature, which seems superimposed on the neg-
ative drifting, is characterized by a centroid frequency of
1/P3 = 0.028 ± 0.001 (P3 = 36 ± 1 periods) with a ten-
dency of the negative phase modulation property.
4.192. J1903+0135
PSR J1903+0135 was discovered by Davies et al. (1973)
using the Mark 1A radio telescope at Jodrell Bank. Sub-
pulse drifting behavior has been reported by Weltevrede et al.
(2006) and Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200813 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.7293 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 245.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of this observation are displayed in Fig. 491. 2DFS
shows a clear drift feature (Fig. 492), and the modulation
frequency with time is wide, which may be caused by the
variable drifting rate, consistent with results in Weltevrede
et al. (2006). Centroid frequencies of the drift feature are
1/P3 = 0.23 ± 0.01 and 1/P2 = 24 ± 1, correspond-
ing to drifting periodicities of P3 = 4.3 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = 15 ± 1◦. However, the top part of the 2DFS is not
double-peaked as described earlier.

4.195. J1903+1728g
PSR J1903+1728g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240516 for 5

utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.6511 s and a disper-

quence is shown in Fig. 500, which displays drifting bands.
Fluctuation spectra are shown in Fig. 501, and there is a
negative drift feature in 2DFS. The centroid frequencies of
the feature are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.090 ± 0.002 and

4.197. J1904+1011
PSR J1904+1011 was discovered by Hulse & Taylor
(1975) using the Arecibo telescope. The pulsar was reported
to have nulling (Lorimer et al. 2002), subpulse drifting (Song
et al. 2023), as well as dwarf pulses (Yan et al. 2024).
Here we report the result of the observation conducted
by FAST on 20230525 for 18 minutes. From this data, a
rotation period P = 1.8564 s and a dispersion measure

Figure 512. Fluctuation analysis of PSR J1905+0758 for the observation

sequences in Fig. 505 show mode changes, especially in the
energy of the leading phase part of the mean pulse proﬁle.
Single pulses of two emission modes are distinguished from
the energy histogram in Fig. 506. where for every single
pulse the energy is integrated over the leading phase inter-
val from −3.9◦to 1.4◦in longitude and the resulting energy
sequence is smoothed with a 7-period moving average. The
two modes are named the leading-weak and leading-strong
modes, labeled in red and green colors, respectively. The po-
larization proﬁles and average PA curves for the two emission
modes are compared in Fig. 507. In the leading-strong mode,
the leading component is stronger than the trailing compo-
nent. The two modes have the same sense of circular polar-
ization, and their PA curves are similar. The ﬂuctuation spec-
tra (Fig. 508) show both negative and positive drift features,
widely distributed over a 1/P3 range of 0 to 0.5. The centroid
frequencies in 1/P2 are −429 ± 2 and 399 ± 4, correspond-
ing to P2 values of −0.838 ± 0.003 degrees and 0.90 ± 0.01
degrees, respectively. These results suggest variable drifting
rates but a relatively stable subpulse phase interval. The low-
frequency modulation feature is likely due to mode changes.
4.199. J1905+0656
PSR J1905+0656 was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200205 for 25
minutes and on 20231001 for 15 minutes.

minute data, a rotation period P = 2.5117 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 24.8 cm−3 pc were determined. Single
pulse sequences of the observation on 20200205 are shown
in Fig. 509. Fluctuation spectra in Fig. 510 reveal a pos-
itive drift feature centered at 1/P3 = 0.041 ± 0.002 and
1/P2 = 86 ± 5, corresponding to P3 = 25 ± 1 periods and

4.202. J1905+1034
PSR was discovered in the PALFA Survey, and previously
reported to have nulling behavior Parent et al. (2021).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200104 for 5
minutes. The single pulse sequence in Fig. 516 illustrates
the existence of nulls. Nulling fraction of this observation
is estimated from the on-pulse integral energy histogram in
Fig. 517 to be 43±3%.
4.203. J1906+0414


## Page 181


181
PSR J1906+0414 was discovered in the Parkes multibeam
pulsar survey (Lorimer et al. 2006).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210507 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0433 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 348.0 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of this observation is shown in Fig. 518.
Fluc-
tuation spectra in Fig. 519 indicate that the leading com-
ponent has two drift features, with centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.202 ± 0.002 (P3 = 4.95 ± 0.04 periods) and
1/P2 = −31±3 (P2 = −12±1◦), and 1/P3 = 0.272±0.001
(P3 = 3.68 ± 0.02 periods) and 1/P2 = −31 ± 3 (P2 =
−12 ± 1◦), respectively. A longer observation is required for
the robust analysis of drift features.

Figure 527.
On-pulse energy histogram of single pulses of PSR
J1908+0457 from the FAST observation on 20210507.
PSR J1906+1854 was discovered by Nice et al. (1995) us-
ing the Arecibo telescope. The subpulse drifting behavior
was reported by Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210802 for 5 min-
utes. The single pulse sequence displayed in Fig. 522 shows
drifting bands. From the LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 523, the
leading and central parts in the mean pulse proﬁle both have
positive subpulse drifting behaviors. For the leading proﬁle
part, 2DFS has a drift feature with centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.063 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 9 ± 2, corresponding
to P3 = 16.0 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 39 ± 8◦. For 2DFS
of the central part, the centroid of the drift feature is char-
acterized by 1/P3 = 0.0637 ± 0.0004 and 1/P2 = 9 ± 3,
yielding drifting periodicities of P3 = 15.7±0.1 periods and
P2 = 39±14◦. However, the drifting property of the trailing
part is not clear. It is likely to be mode changing or nulling
of pulses No.135-140. Further observations are needed to
conﬁrm this behavior.
4.206. J1907+0709g
PSR J1907+0709g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201107 and
20250807, each for 15 minutes. From the observation on
20201107, a rotation period P = 0.3441 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 280.1 cm−3 pc were derived. The single
pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 200-400
from the data on 20201107 are shown in Fig. 524. In the
ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 525, there is a modulation feature
with the centroid at 1/P3 = 0.479 ± 0.001, corresponding
to P3 = 2.089 ± 0.003 periods. The modulation feature ob-
served on 20250807 is consistent with that on 20201107.
4.207. J1908+0457
PSR J1908+0457 was discovered in a search targeted at
Einstein IPC X-ray sources using the Arecibo telescope
(Zepka et al. 1996).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210507 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.8467 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 349.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence is displayed in Fig. 526, which illustrates the ex-
istence of nulls. The nulling fraction is estimated from the
on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 527) to be 26±1%.

4.208. J1908+0916
PSR J1908+0916 was discovered by Hulse & Taylor
(1975) using the Arecibo telescope. The subpulse drifting of
the pulsar was reported by Song et al. (2023) to be P3=24(5)
periods and P2=17+66
−4 degrees.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20191226 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.8303 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 250.8 cm−3 pc.
The single
pulse sequence is displayed in Fig. 528. Drifting parame-
ters of the leading and trailing parts in a mean pulse proﬁle
are estimated from ﬂuctuation spectra (Fig. 529). The cen-
troid frequencies of the drift feature in 2DFS are 1/P3 =
0.044 ± 0.001 (P3 = 22.8 ± 0.4 periods) and 1/P2 = 11 ± 1
(P2 = 33 ± 3◦) for the leading proﬁle part, and 1/P3 =
0.058 ± 0.002 (P3 = 17.4 ± 0.5 periods) and 1/P2 = 12 ± 2
(P2 = 31 ± 4◦) for the trailing part.

20250209, a rotation period P = 0.5116 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 255.1 cm−3 pc were determined. Single
pulse sequences of the observation on 20250209 in Fig. 532
display changes between different states, as well as nega-
tive subpulse drifting in the bright emission state. To dis-
tinguish the two states and reduce the inﬂuence of intensity
variations in the bright emission state, the sequence of on-
pulse integral energies for single pulses is smoothed using
a 17-period moving average. The histogram of smoothed
energy sequence is shown in Fig. 533, where the weak and
bright emission modes of single pulses are distinguished,
with the averaged proﬁle of two emission states displayed
in Fig. 534. The negative drift feature in 2DFS (Fig. 535) is
temporally widely distributed, with the centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.074 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −4.3 ± 0.2, cor-
responding to periodicities of P3 = 13.6 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = −84±4◦. The single pulse behavior of the observation
on 20250523 is consistent with those on 20250209.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230214 for 44
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.3213 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 296.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 548 show the existence of the nulling
phenomenon. The on-pulse energy histogram is displayed
in Fig. 549, where the energy values have been averaged
over every four periods to improve the emission signiﬁcance
(Wang et al. 2020). From the energy histogram, the nulling
fraction is estimated to be 24±3%.

PSR J1910+1231 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Hulse & Taylor 1975).
This pulsar was observed by FAST for 4 minutes, deriv-
ing a rotation period P = 1.4416 s and a dispersion mea-
sure DM = 257.8 cm−3 pc. The single pulse sequence in
Fig. 550 illustrates that there is the nulling phenomenon. The
nulling fraction of this observation is estimated to be 15±4%
from the on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 551).
4.218. J1910-0219g
PSR J1910-0219g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250619 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.5316 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 185.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 552 show the nulling phenomenon. The
nulling fraction of this observation is 17.8±2.2%, which is
estimated from the on-pulse energy histogram in Fig. 553.

Figure 555. Single pulse sequences of PSR J1910-0309 from the FAST
observation on 20250317.
Fluctuation spectra in Fig. 554 illustrate the existence of neg-
ative drifting. Centroid frequencies of the negative drift fea-
ture in 2DFS are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.470 ± 0.002
and 1/P2 = −80 ± 5, corresponding to periodicities of
P3 = 2.13 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −4.5 ± 0.3 degrees.
4.219. J1910-0309
PSR J1910-0309 was discovered in the second Molonglo
pulsar survey (Manchester et al. 1978), exhibiting the sub-
pulse drifting phenomenon of P3 = 22 ± 14 periods and
P2 = −127+107
−86 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250317 for 6 min-
utes, with a rotation period P = 0.5046 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 205.5 cm−3 pc derived. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 555.
LRFS and 2DFS of the
leading, central, and trailing parts in a mean pulse proﬁle
(Fig. 556) illustrate that the temporally modulated frequen-
cies are widely distributed. The centroid frequencies for the
modulation features of three proﬁle parts are estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.119 ± 0.004, 0.051 ± 0.003, and 0.083 ± 0.002,
corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 8.4±0.3, 20±1, and
12.1 ± 0.3 periods.

PSR J1911+1440g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240125 and
20250326, each for a duration of 15 minutes. From the data
of 20240125, a rotation period P = 0.5825 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 86.9 cm−3 pc were derived. The sin-
gle pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view of this observation
in Fig. 557 display the behavior of short-duration emission
enhanced. From the on-pulse integral energy histogram in
Fig. 558, the nulling fraction is estimated to be 66.9±4.4%.
The emission duration is short, typically 1 period but rang-
ing up to 3, while nulls last from 1 to 49 periods (Fig. 559).
Histograms in Fig. 560 show that the nulling degree is large,
to be 80±12 degrees. The nulling scale is estimated to be

4.226. J1913+1011
PSR J1913+1011 was discovered in the Parkes Multibeam
Pulsar Survey Morris et al. (2002).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201206 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.0359 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 178.6 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 571. From LRFS and 2DFS of
the on-pulse region (Fig. 572), there is a temporally low-
frequency modulation feature with the centroid frequency of
1/P3 = 0.0183 ± 0.0004 (P3 = 55 ± 1 periods), as well
as a negative drift feature with the centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.1119 ± 0.0004 (P3 = 8.94 ± 0.03 periods) and
1/P2 = −5.3 ± 0.3 (P2 = −67 ± 4◦).
4.227. J1913+11025
PSR J1913+11025 was discovered by the Arecibo tele-
scope (Lazarus et al. 2015). Parent et al. (2022) reported that
this pulsar displays nulling behavior at 1.4 GHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250823 for 15
minutes, and a rotation period P = 0.9239 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 624.2 cm−3 pc were determined. The
single pulse sequence in Fig. 573 displays the nulling phe-
nomenon. To improve the signiﬁcance of pulses in the his-
togram (Fig. 574), the on-pulse energies are averaged for ev-
ery four pulses using the method described in Wang et al.
(2020). The nulling fraction of this observation is estimated
to be 28.8±1.5%.

utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.8511 s and a disper-

in Fig. 577 show occasional nulling behavior. The nulling

preferred negative drifting for the leading part in the mean
pulse proﬁle, from LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 577. The drift

4.229. J1914+0219
PSR J1914+0219 was discovered by Lorimer et al. (2013)
in the Parkes Multibeam Pulsar Survey. Subpulse drifting
was reported by Song et al. (2023) with P3 = 6.2 ± 0.8
periods and P2 = 18+2
−3 degrees.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240927 for 15
minutes, with a rotation period P = 0.4576 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 235.3 cm−3 pc determined. Sin-
gle pulse sequences in Fig. 578 display subpulse drifting and
the short-duration decreases in pulse energy. In 2DFS of the
leading part in the mean pulse proﬁle, there is a positive drift
feature with centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.166 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = 19 ± 1, corresponding to drift periodicities of
P3 = 6.03 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = 19 ± 1 degrees. While
there is no obvious evidence of drifting for the right proﬁle
part from this data.

sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No.

4.231. J1914+1122
PSR J1914+1122 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Hulse & Taylor 1975).
The drift feature of the second
component has been reported by Song et al. (2023), with
P3 = 6 ± 1 periods and P2 = −15+9
−9 degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200304 for 5 min-
utes and 20220513 for 15 minutes. From the 15-minute data,
a rotation period P = 0.6010 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 100.2 cm−3 pc are derived. The single pulse se-
quence and a zoomed-in view are shown in Fig. 583. From
the ﬂuctuation in Fig. 584, the trailing part in a mean pulse
proﬁle has a negative drift feature, with the centroid frequen-
cies estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.169 ± 0.004 and 1/P2 =
−29 ± 3.
Periodicities of P3 = 5.9 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = −13 ± 1 degrees are consistent with previous studies
(Song et al. 2023).

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201229 for 5
minutes and on 20250813 for 15 minutes. From the longer
data, a rotation period P = 0.3173 s and a dispersion mea-
sure DM = 192.1 cm−3 pc were derived. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 1000-1300
from the observation on 20250813 (Fig. 585) illustrate the
presence of subpulse modulation for the leading proﬁle part.
LRFS and 2DFS of the leading part are shown in Fig. 586.
The centroid of the negative drift feature in 2DFS is at
1/P3 = 0.110 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −2.5 ± 0.6, correspond-
ing to P3 = 9.1 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −146 ± 35 degrees.
4.233. J1915+0738
PSR J1915+0738 was discovered using the 305 m radio
telescope at Arecibo (Nice et al. 1995). Drifting parameters
of P3 = 37(40) periods and P2 = 14+2
−10 degrees were re-
ported by Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211010,
20211208, and 20220117, with each observation conducted
for a duration of 15 minutes. From the data of 20211010,
a rotation period P
= 1.5428 s and a dispersion mea-
sure DM = 38.0 cm−3 pc were determined. Single pulse
sequences of the observation on 20211010 are shown in
Fig. 587, where subpulse drifting is not systematic and some-
times changes directions. There is also a modulation of about
2 periods.
Fluctuation spectra are displayed in Fig. 588,
and there are two drift features as well as a modulation fea-
ture. The main feature in 2DFS is the positive drift feature,
which has the centroid frequencies 1/P3 = 0.027 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = 87 ± 4, yielding P3 = 37 ± 1 periods and
P2 = 4.1 ± 0.2◦. The negative drift feature exhibits the cen-
troid of 1/P3 = 0.025 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −71 ± 6, corre-
sponding to P3 = 40 ± 2 periods and P2 = −5.0 ± 0.4◦.
There is also a modulation feature with the centroid of
1/P3 = 0.450 ± 0.002, that corresponds to the periodicity
of P3 = 2.22 ± 0.01 periods. For the other two observations,
the drifting properties are consistent with 20211010, while
there is no obvious modulation feature of 2 periods in 2DFS.

4.234. J1915+1009
PSR J1915+1009 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Hulse & Taylor 1975). Negative drift feature with P3 =
4.2 ± 0.4 periods and P2 = −18+7
−9 degrees was reported by
Song et al. (2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200306 and
20211118 for 5 minutes. From the data of 20200306, a rota-
tion period P = 0.4045 s and a dispersion measure DM =
241.7 cm−3 pc were determined. Single pulse sequences of
the observation on 20200306 are displayed in Fig. 589. Fluc-
tuation spectra in Fig. 590 exhibit a negative drift feature,
with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.233 ± 0.002 and
1/P2 = −44 ± 2, corresponding to drifting periodicities of
P3 = 4.30 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = −8.2 ± 0.4◦.

pulsar survey (Hobbs et al. 2004). The nulling of this pul-

sar was ﬁrst reported by Wang et al. (2007) with a nulling
fraction of 47% at 1518MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200301 for 5
minutes, 20201230 for 20 minutes, 20210420 for 28 min-
utes, and 20220202 for 15 minutes. From data of 20210420,
a rotation period P = 1.6182 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 341.0 cm−3 pc were determined. Four segments are
shown in Fig. 591. From these sensitive FAST observations,
the pulsar is found to have different drifting modes, in addi-
tion to the nulling behavior.
4.235.1. Nulling
The nulling fraction of the observation on 20210420 is es-
timated to be 47±5% from the on-pulse energy histogram
(Fig. 592). Distributions of duration for adjacent nulling and
emission of this observation are shown in Fig. 593. From sin-
gle pulse sequences in Fig. 591, there are short-duration nulls
occurring in drifting bands, as well as short-duration emis-
sion in the nulling state. The 63th single pulse of the obser-
vation on 20200301 (Fig. 591) displays partial null. The mo-
ment when nulling ends and emission begins corresponds to
a position within the third subpulse, indicating that the short-
duration nulls of this pulsar are not caused by the carousel
rotation. The duration of emission ranging from 1 to 22 pe-
riods with an average of 5 periods, that is shorter than the
duration of nulling state ranging from 1 to 103 periods with
an average of 24 periods. From the histogram in Fig. 594,
the nulling degree and scale are estimated to be 55±32 de-
grees and 27±29 periods for NE pairs. For EN pairs, they
are 58±28 degrees and 26±29 periods.
There is a also dwarf pulse appearing in the nulling state,
that is the pulse No.327 in the observation on 20210420
(Fig. 591).

4.235.2. Drifting and mode changing
There are three drifting modes from the FAST observa-
tions, labeled by bars with different colors in Fig. 591. Drift-
ing of two modes is relatively stable, and drifting parameters
are calculated from the correlation method (Fig. 595). The
drifting rate of one mode changes continuously from slow to
fast. For most drifting bands, the drifting rate is stable and
slow with drifting rate of −0.7 degrees per period and P2 of
8.7◦on average, which is deﬁned as Sd mode (red bars in
Fig. 591). The segment of pulse Nos. 457-550 in the left
bottom of Fig. 591 from the observation on 20201230 shows
that the emission begins with a very slow drifting rate, slower
than that of the Sd mode. The drifting rate is faster and faster
continually, eventually stable at the fastest drifting rate. The
state with stable fast drifting rate is deﬁned as Fd mode in yel-
low color, with the drifting parameters P2 = −8.03 ± 0.01◦
and D = −1.19 ± 0.06 degrees per period estimated from

Figure 607.
Mean polarization proﬁles (the top panel) for the normal
(red) and abnormal (green) emission modes of PSR J1919+0021 observed
on 20210702, as well as the averaged PA curves (the bottom panel). Proﬁles
in the top panel are normalized by their respective peaks.
PSR J1919+0021 was discovered by the Mark 1A radio
telescope at Jodrell Bank (Davies et al. 1973). Rankin (1986)
reported the pulsar with the mode-changing phenomenon that
the core component was enhanced predominantly relative to
the outer component. Weltevrede et al. (2006, 2007) detected
broad drifting behavior at 21 cm and 92 cm, and amplitude
modulation was reported by Basu et al. (2016) at 333 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210702 for 6 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.2723 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 89.6 cm−3 pc. The single-pulse se-
quence in Fig. 605 shows that the pulsar exhibits a mode
changing phenomenon in which the emission is sometimes
enhanced. In the histogram of smoothed on-pulse integral
energy variation every 3 single pulses (Fig. 606), two emis-
sion modes can be easily distinguished, which are labeled by
red and green colors. In the normal mode, the leading com-
ponent is the brightest. The emission on-pulse phase region,
especially for the central component, is enhanced in the ab-
normal mode (Fig. 607), which is consistent with the result
of Rankin (1986).
Further observations are needed for the modulation analy-
sis.
4.241. J1919+0134
PSR J1919+0134 was discovered in a survey of intermedi-
ate Galactic latitudes using the Parkes 64-m radio telescope
and shown to have regular drift (Edwards et al. 2001). Drift-
ing parameters were previously reported to be P3 ∼6.5 pe-
riods and P2 ∼9◦(Ord et al. 2001; Basu et al. 2016; Song
et al. 2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220310 and
20230328 each for 15 minutes.
A rotation period P

were determined from the observation on 20230328. Sin-
gle pulse sequences of two observations in Fig. 608 display
nulling and mode changing between different subpulse drift-

On-pulse energy histograms of single pulses of PSR

ing modes. It’s the ﬁrst time revealing nulling and the drift
rate changingfrom the highly sensitive FAST observations.
Based on the on-pulse integral energy histograms in
Fig. 609, the nulling fractions of the observations on
20220310 and 20230328 are 3.3±0.5% and 3.3±0.3%, re-
spectively.
We distinguish three drifting modes on the basis of differ-
ent subpulse drift features and calculate the drifting param-
eters using the correlation method. They are normal drift-
ing mode, fast drifting (Fd) mode, and slow drifting (Sd)
mode, where Fd and Sd modes are newly distinguished.
The most frequent drifting mode is the normal mode, la-
beled in red. The drifting parameters of this mode are esti-
mated to be P2 = −7.34 ± 0.01◦and D = −1.08 ± 0.04
degrees per period for the observation on 20220310, and
P2 = −7.43 ± 0.01◦and D = −1.09 ± 0.06 degrees per
period for the observation on 20230328, which is consistent
with previous studies (Ord et al. 2001; Basu et al. 2016; Song
et al. 2023). The fast drifting mode is labeled by blue color,
with P2 = −6.80 ± 0.01◦and D = −1.51 ± 0.03 degrees
per period (20220310), and P2 = −6.88 ± 0.01◦and D =
−1.43±0.04 degrees per period (20230328). The slow drift-
ing mode labeled with yellow color has P2 = −6.78 ± 0.01◦
and D = −0.59 ± 0.05 degrees per period (20220310), and
P2 = −6.75 ± 0.01◦and D = −0.80 ± 0.05 degrees per
period (20230328). The slow drifting mode is minimally oc-
curring in these two observations.

4.243. J1919+1527g
PSR J1919+1527g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230214 for 46
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.3713 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 695.0 cm−3 pc. From single
pulse sequences in Fig. 613, the pulsar has the bright and
weak emission modes, as demonstrated from the asymmetric
distribution around the zero in the on-pulse integral energy
histogram (Fig. 614). Bright and weak emission modes are
labeled using green and red colors. Averaged polarization
proﬁles and PA curves of two emission modes are shown in
Fig. 615.
4.244. J1919+2621
PSR J1919+2621 was discovered by FAST (Cameron et al.
2020).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210801 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.6515 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 96.3 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of the observation are shown in Fig. 616, which dis-
play unsystematic subpulse drifting phenomenon. From ﬂuc-
tuation spectra in Fig. 617, this pulsar has a preferred nega-
tive drifting direction. The centroid frequencies of the drift
feature in 2DFS are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.417 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −25 ± 2, which correspond to periodicities of
P3 = 2.40 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −14 ± 1◦.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230510 for 96
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.9566 s and a dis-

persion measure DM = 102.4 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of this observation and three zoomed-in views are
shown in Fig. 618, illustrating the existence of nulling, as

From the on-pulse integral energy histogram in Fig. 620,
the nulling fraction of this observation is estimated to be
66±6%. The distribution of continuous period numbers for
adjacent nulling and emission states is shown in Fig. 621,
where the duration of the nulling state is 91±87 periods, and
that of the emission state is 6±5 periods. From histograms in
Fig. 622, nulling degree and scale for NE pairs are 79±17 de-
grees and 90±85 periods, and those for EN pairs are 79±16
degrees and 92±87 periods, indicating a signiﬁcantly longer
duration of the nulling state compared to the emission state.
This pulsar also displays subpulse drifting behavior, as
zoomed-in views of the single pulse sequence in Fig. 618.
Notably, the drifting properties change in different con-
tinuous emission segments, and the drifting direction also
changes occasionally. For instance, the drifting rate and di-
rection change in the segment between pulses No.

in Fig. 626 show the subpulse modulation behavior for both

PSR J1921+2153 was discovered by Hewish et al. (1968)
at 81.5 MHz at the Mullard Radio Astronomy Observa-
tory. The pulsar has been reported with a nulling fraction
of ≤0.25% by Ritchings (1976) at 408 MHz and ≤1.2%
by Vivekanand (1995) at 326.5 MHz. Drifting phenomenon
and phase step of this pulsar have been reported (e.g., Backer
1970c; Proszynski & Wolszczan 1986; Weltevrede et al.
2006, 2007; Basu et al. 2016, 2019a, etc).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200419 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.3372 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 11.6 cm−3 pc. However, there is
no nulling phenomenon from the single pulse sequences in

persion measure DM = 234.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 100-350 in
Fig. 635 show that the leading and trailing parts of a mean
pulse proﬁle exhibit the intensity modulation and the positive
drifting behavior, respectively. Fluctuation spectra are dis-
played in Fig. 636. For the leading proﬁle part, the centroid
frequency of the modulation feature in 2DFS is estimated to
be 1/P3 = 0.106 ± 0.003, corresponding to P3 = 9.4 ± 0.3
periods. 2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part exhibits a positive
drift feature, and the centroid is characterized by frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.124 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 3 ± 1, yielding the
periodicities of P3 = 8.0 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 130 ± 55
degrees.
4.252. J1922+2018
PSR J1922+2018 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Hulse & Taylor 1975).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210208 and
20210220, both for 5 minutes.
From the observation on
20210208, a rotation period P = 1.1728 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 203.8 cm−3 pc were determined. Here we
present the analysis of the observation on 20210208, which
is similar to that on 20210220. The single pulse sequence
observed by FAST on 20210208 is displayed in Fig. 637.
The leading and trailing parts in the proﬁle seem to have
systematic modulation. The former is more likely to have

Figure 637. Single pulse sequence of PSR J1922+2018 from the FAST
observation on 20210208.
PSR J1922+2110 was discovered by the Mark 1A radio
telescope at Jodrell Bank (Davies et al. 1973). Weltevrede
et al. (2007) reported three sharp features with 0.19, 0.24,
and 0.28 cycle per period (cpp) in LRFS and 2DFS at 92cm,
and they found the 0.24 cpp feature is in the ﬁrst half of the
data and the other two in the last half.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210706 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0779 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 217.1 cm−3 pc. The data of two
beams, P1M07 and P4M07, are used to do the analysis. Sin-
gle pulse sequences in Fig. 639 show that there are two emis-
sion modes, as well as the modulation phenomenon.
The energy series of the central component is smoothed
every 5 periods. The normal and abnormal modes are distin-
guished from this smoothed energy histogram of the central
component in Fig. 640, which are labeled in red and green,
respectively. There are two segments of the abnormal mode
in Fig. 639.
Averaged polarization proﬁles of two emis-
sion modes, as well as PA curves, are displayed in Fig. 641.
The intensity of the central part is strongest in the normal
mode, while the leading component is the brightest in the ab-
normal mode. The most prominent difference between PA
curves of two emission modes is the orthogonal modes in
the longitude phase range between -4 and -2 degrees. In ad-
dition, there is a preferred positive drifting for the trailing
proﬁle part of the normal mode. 2DFS shown in Fig. 642

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20191226 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.2378 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 226.6 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 1000-1200
in Fig. 654 illustrate the modulation behavior and the pres-
ence of quasi-periodic subpulses. Fluctuation spectra are dis-
played in Fig. 655, and the centroid frequency of the main
modulation feature is 1/P3 = 0.071 ± 0.001, corresponding
to P3 = 14.2 ± 0.3 periods. In addition, symmetrically lo-
cated secondary peaks are present in the bottom subpanel of
the 2DFS. The P2 values of −3.9±0.1 and 3.8±0.1 degrees
are derived from the centroid frequencies 1/P2 of −93 ± 1
and 95±2, respectively. These values correspond to the phase
interval of the quasi-periodic subpulses.

4.261. J1926+1434
PSR J1926+1434 was discovered by the Arecibo tele-
scope (Hulse & Taylor 1974). Drifting parameters were pre-
sented by Song et al. (2023): P3 = 17 ± 11 periods and
P2 = −100+52
−28 degrees for the leading component, and
P3 = 23 ± 4 periods and P2 = −149+69
−22 degrees for the
trailing component.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210317 for 15
minutes, driving a rotation period P = 1.3248 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 211.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 666 show nulling and unsystematic subpulse drifting
phenomena. The nulling fraction of this observation is esti-
mated to be 0.6±0.3% from the on-pulse energy histogram
in Fig. 667. Fluctuation spectra are shown in Fig. 668. In
the 2DFS of the leading component of the mean pulse pro-
ﬁle, two negative drift features are present: one with centroid
frequencies 1/P3 = 0.026 ± 0.001 (P3 = 38 ± 1 periods)
and 1/P2 = −6 ± 1 (P2 = −57 ± 13 degrees), and the other
with 1/P3 = 0.134 ± 0.001 (P3 = 7.47 ± 0.04 periods) and
1/P2 = −10 ± 1 (P2 = −36 ± 4 degrees). 2DFS of the
trailing proﬁle part exhibits a preferred negative drift feature,
with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.035 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −4±1, corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 28±1
periods and P2 = −86 ± 22 degrees.
4.262. J1926+1631g
PSR J1926+1631g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211226 for 15
minutes and on 20250214 for 20 minutes. From the obser-
vation on 20211226, a rotation period P = 0.6784 s and
a dispersion measure DM = 196.7 cm−3 pc were deter-
mined. Single-pulse features of two observations are consis-
tent. Single pulse sequences of the observation on 20211226
in Fig. 669 illustrate that the pulsar has subpulse drifting be-
havior.
From 2DFS in Fig. 670, the centroid frequencies
are 1/P3 = 0.174 ± 0.001 (P3 = 5.76 ± 0.02 periods)
and 1/P2 = −45 ± 4 (P2 = −8 ± 1◦) for the leading
part of a mean pulse proﬁle, and 1/P3 = 0.153 ± 0.002

utes, and a rotation period P = 0.2534 s and a dispersion

weak and bright emission modes of single pulses are distin-
guished from the histogram of smoothed energies (Fig. 674),
which are labeled in red and green. Mean polarization pro-
ﬁles and the averaged PA curves of two modes are shown in
Fig. 675, and the weak emission mode has a wider proﬁle.
