PSR J1927+0911 was discovered in the Parkes 20-cm
multibeam pulsar survey of the Galactic plane (Lorimer et al.
2006). The negative subpulse drifting was reported by Song
et al. (2023) with P3=23±5 periods and P2=-7.0+0.6
−5
degrees.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201220 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.2903 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 202.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 676 display drifting bands.
Fluctuation
spectra shown in Fig. 677 exhibit a negative drift feature,
with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.042 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −51±1, that correspond to P3 = 23.6±0.3 periods
and P2 = −7.1 ± 0.1◦. From the single pulse sequences in

using the Parkes Radio Telescope.

utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.3660 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 124.1 cm−3 pc. From the single pulse
sequence in Fig. 681 and the on-pulse integral energy his-
togram in Fig. 682, the pulsar has the nulling phenomenon,
with a fraction of this observation estimated to be 24±4%.

4.268. J1928+0840g
PSR J1928+0840g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250810 for 5 min-
utes and 20251019 for 15 minutes. From the 15-minute data,
a rotation period P = 1.7835 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 121.7 cm−3 pc were determined. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 150-340 are

shown in Figure 683. The distribution around zero energy in
the on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 684) indicates

necessary for further analysis of the nulling behavior.

minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.8173 s and a

gle pulse sequences of these two observations in Fig. 692
illustrate that subpulses of this pulsar mainly negatively drift,
sometimes changing drifting direction and displaying a "C"-
shape pattern.
Fluctuation spectra of these two observa-
tions are shown in Fig. 693. The modulation frequency over
time of the negatively drifting feature is widely distributed
in 2DFS. For the main drift feature in 2DFS of the observa-
tion on 20190329, centroid frequencies are estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.068 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −5.2 ± 0.6, yielding
periodicities of P3 = 14.7±0.2 periods and P2 = −69±8◦.

4.275. J1931+1439
PSR J1931+1439 was discovered in the Arecibo Pulsar-
ALFA (PALFA) survey (Lazarus et al. 2015).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240129 for 30
minutes and 20240917 for 15 minutes. From the longer ob-
servation, a rotation period P = 1.7793 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 237.5 cm−3 pc were derived.
Here we
present the analysis of the data on 20240129. Single pulse
sequences are shown in Fig. 699, displaying nulling as well
as modulation behaviors. The nulling fraction is estimated
from the on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 700) to be
39±3%.
Fluctuation spectra are shown in Fig. 701.
For
the leading part in the mean pulse proﬁle, there is a pre-
ferred negative drift feature with centroid frequencies esti-
mated to be 1/P3 = 0.463 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −39 ± 4 in
2DFS, corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 2.16 ± 0.01
periods and P2 = −9 ± 1◦.
2DFS of the trailing pro-
ﬁle part exhibits a modulation feature with the centroid of
1/P3 = 0.462 ± 0.001, yielding P3 = 2.17 ± 0.01 periods.

4.276. J1931+30
PSR J1931+30 was discovered by Camilo et al. (1996a)
using the Arecibo radio telescope at 430 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210701 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.5821 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 53.3 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 702, where the drift bands are not
systematic. From LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 703, the mod-
ulation frequency with time is wide, with the centroid of
1/P3 = 0.077 ± 0.001 (P3 = 13.0 ± 0.2 periods) and
1/P2 = −14 ± 1 (P2 = −26 ± 2 degrees).
4.277. J1931-0144
PSR J1931-0144 was discovered by FAST (Cameron et al.
2020; You et al. 2021).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20221120 for 5 min-
utes, and a rotation period P = 0.5937 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 37.7 cm−3 pc were derived. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 704 show the subpulse drifting phenomenon.
From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 705, centroid frequencies
of the positive drift feature are 1/P3 = 0.109 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = 24 ± 1, corresponding to periodicities of P3 =
9.2 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 15 ± 1 degrees.
4.278. J1932+1500
PSR J1932+1500 was discovered in the Arecibo L-band
Feed Array pulsar survey Lyne et al. (2017).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210606 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.8641 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 91.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence shown in Fig. 706 displays the nulling phenomenon.
The nulling fraction of this observation is estimated from the
on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 707) to be 38±3%.
4.279. J1932+2220
PSR J1932+2220 was discovered by Hulse & Taylor
(1975) using the Arecibo telescope. Song et al. (2023) re-
ported a P3-only feature with P3 = 41 ± 8 periods.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230707 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.1445 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 218.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 708 show a modulation signature. From the ﬂuctua-
tion in Fig. 709, the low-frequency modulation feature has
a centroid at 1/P3 = 0.0261 ± 0.0004, corresponding to
P3 = 38 ± 1 periods. This value is consistent with the P3
reported by Song et al. (2023).
4.280. J1933+1304
PSR J1933+1304 was discovered by Hulse & Taylor
(1975) using the Arecibo telescope. From previous work,
there are 4.92-period and 6.6-period modulations common to
both components Rankin et al. (2023); Song et al. (2023).
The observation of the FAST on 20201220 for 5 minutes,
deriving a rotation period P = 0.9284 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 176.3 cm−3 pc. Single-pulse behavior of
the FAST observation is consistent with previous studies.
Single pulse sequences in Fig. 710 display the modulation
behavior.
The ﬂuctuation spectra are shown in Fig. 711,
and there are two features in 2DFS for both the leading and
trailing parts in the mean pulse proﬁle. For 2DFS of the
leading proﬁle part, there is a negative subpulse drift fea-
ture with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.207 ± 0.001
(P3 = 4.83 ± 0.02 periods) and 1/P2 = −37 ± 7 (P2 =
−10 ± 2◦), as well as an amplitude variation feature without
any signiﬁcant phase modulation with the centroid frequency
of 1/P3 = 0.151 ± 0.001 (P3 = 6.61 ± 0.03◦). 2DFS of the
trailing part also exhibits a negative drift feature with the cen-
troid of 1/P3 = 0.2076±0.0004 (P3 = 4.82±0.01 periods)


## Page 240


240
and 1/P2 = −43±5 (P2 = −8±1◦), and a modulation fea-
ture with the centroid characterized by 1/P3 = 0.145±0.001
(P3 = 6.91 ± 0.03 periods). Additionally, there are 4 single
pulses whose on-pulse integral energies are less than 3σE,
which seems to be related to short-duration nulling behavior.

this pulsar has nulling and modulation phenomena. Long
nulls were shown by Brinkman et al. (2018). Song et al.
(2023) reported drift feature of the leading component and
P3-only feature of the trailing component.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210618 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.9395 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 189.6 cm−3 pc.
Nulling be-
havior is apparent in the single pulse sequence (Fig. 712),
and the nulling fraction is estimated to be 38.2% from the
integral energy histogram of the longitude range between -
6.0◦and 3.2◦(Fig. 713). In 2DFS of the leading part in the
mean pulse proﬁle (Fig. 714), there is a negative drift feature
with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.139 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −14 ± 2, corresponding to P3 = 7.2 ± 0.1 periods
and P2 = −25 ± 3◦.
4.282. J1935+1616
PSR J1935+1616 was discovered by the Jodrell Bank Mk
I telescope at 408 MHz (Davies & Large 1970). The nulling
fraction was estimated to be less than 0.25% at 408 MHz by
Ritchings (1976) and less than 0.06% at 645 MHz by Biggs
(1992). From previous studies, this pulsar also has a mod-
ulation behavior. Backer (1973) reported the pulsar with a
broad short-period feature without a preferred drift direction
at 430 MHz. Oster et al. (1977) showed drifting at 430 MHz,
and preferred positive drifting in a broad feature near the P3=
2 periods alias border of 2DFS was reported by Weltevrede
et al. (2006, 2007) from the 21-cm and 92-cm data.
This pulsar has been observed by FAST on 20190919 for
53 minutes and 20211117 for 52 minutes. From the data of
20190919, a rotation period P = 0.3588 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 158.5 cm−3 pc were estimated. FAST data
illustrate that this pulsar has mode changing and modulation

lagged relative to the normal mode. For the weak mode, the
part of the longitude between -5◦and -1◦in the proﬁle is
much weaker than the part of -1◦to 5◦. PA of two wings is
consistent between two emission states, while it is different
in the longitude range between -5◦and 2◦.

4.283. J1937+1358g
PSR J1937+1358g was discovered by (Han et al. 2021,
2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210814 for 15
minutes. The single pulse sequence of this observation is
shown in Fig. 720. The pulsar has both nulling and sub-
pulse drifting behaviors. The nulling fraction of the observa-
tion is estimated from the on-pulse integral energy histogram
(Fig. 721) to be 43±0.02%. The LRFS and 2DFS for pulses
175-230 are presented in Fig. 722 to do the ﬂuctuation anal-
ysis and decrease the effect of nulls. 2DFS of the leading
part in the mean pulse proﬁle has a negative drifting fea-
ture, with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.074 ± 0.003
and 1/P2 = −15 ± 5, corresponding to periodicities of
P3 = 13.5±0.5 periods and P2 = −24±7◦. For the trailing
proﬁle part, the centroid of the drift feature is characterized
by 1/P3 = 0.063 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −16 ± 4, yielding
P3 = 16.0 ± 0.6 periods and P2 = −22 ± 5◦.

Figure 725. Fluctuation analysis of PSR J1937+1505 from the FAST ob-
servation on 20201226, with LRFS and 2DFS for the leading part of a mean
pulse proﬁle.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201226 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.8730 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 228.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence is displayed in Fig. 723, where the pulsar is newly
found to have nulling and subpulse modulation behaviors.
The nulling fraction is estimated to be 38±4% from the on-
pulse integral energy histogram. From the LRFS and 2DFS
in Fig. 725, there is a temporal modulation feature with the
centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.471 ± 0.003 for the leading
part of the proﬁle, which corresponds to P3 = 2.12±0.01 pe-
riods. However, limited to the high-percent nulling and short
pulse numbers, the accurate modulation parameters require
further observation.
4.285. J1937+2544
PSR J1937+2544 was found by (Dewey et al. 1985) using
the 92 m telescope at Green Bank.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210623 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.2010 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 53.2 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence of the observation and the zoomed view of pulses
No. 390-730 are shown in Fig. 726. In 2DFS of the trail-
ing proﬁle part (Fig. 727), the preferred direction of the
main drift feature is positive, with the centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.104±0.001 and 1/P2 = 21 ±1, corresponding
to P3 = 9.6 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 18 ± 1◦.

utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.6573 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 112.7 cm−3 pc. The single pulse

PSR J1938+0650 was discovered by Foster et al. (1995)
using the Arecibo radio telescope.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210702 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.1215 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 74.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
are shown in Fig. 730. The pulsar is found to have the nulling
phenomenon, and the nulling fraction of this observation is
estimated to be 12±1% from the on-pulse integral energy
histogram in Fig. 731. Single pulse sequences display some
occasional drifting bands, such as pulse Nos. 102-115, al-
though the drifting behavior is not systematic and always
appears. Using the cross-correlation method (Fig. 732), the
drifting parameters are estimated to be D = 0.27 ± 0.18 de-
grees per period and P2 = 2.83 ± 0.04◦for these drifting
bands.

PSR J1938+14 was discovered in the Arecibo 327 MHz

2016).
J1938+14 was observed by FAST on 20201226 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.9028 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 75.6 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence in Fig. 733 indicates nulling and subpulse drifting
of this pulsar. The nulling fraction is estimated to be 9±1%
from the on-pulse integral energy histogram in Fig. 734. Cen-
troid frequencies of the main feature in 2DFS (Fig. 735) are
1/P3 = 0.148±0.002 and 1/P2 = 25±4, which correspond
to periodicities of P3 = 6.8 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 15 ± 3◦.

4.291. J1939+2352g
PSR J1939+2352g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240317 for 20
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.1451 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 413.5 cm−3 pc. The nulling fraction
is estimated to be 52.6% from the on-pulse integral energy
histogram in Fig. 743. Additionally, there is also drifting be-
havior from the single pulse (Fig. 742) and ﬂuctuation spec-
tra (Fig. 744). In 2DFS, there is a positive drift feature with
the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.245 ± 0.004 and 1/P2 = 61 ± 3,
corresponding to periodicities of P3 = 4.1 ± 0.1 periods and
P2=5.9±0.3 degrees.
4.292. J1939+2453g
PSR J1939+2453g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210514 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.4024 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 226.8 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence of this observation is displayed in Fig. 745, which
shows changes between nulling and emission states.
The
nulling fraction of this observation is estimated to be 76±4%
from the on-pulse integral energy histogram in Fig. 746.

lution Universe survey by Morello et al. (2019).

Figure 747. Single pulse sequence of PSR J1940+0239 from the FAST
observation on 20210719.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210719 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.2322 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 86.1 cm−3 pc. We ﬁrst report the sub-
pulse behavior of the pulsar here. In single pulse sequences
(Fig. 747), drift bands of the trailing component are clear.
LRFS and 2DFS of three longitude parts in the mean pulse
proﬁle are displayed in Fig. 748. For the leading proﬁle part,
2DFS exhibits a preferred positive drift feature with the cen-
troid of 1/P3 = 0.153 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 28 ± 12, cor-
responding to P3 = 6.5 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 13 ± 6◦.
For the trailing proﬁle part, there is also a positive drift fea-
ture in 2DFS, with the centroid characterized by 1/P3 =
0.148 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 33 ± 4, yielding P3 = 6.7 ± 0.1
periods and P2 = 11 ± 1◦. 2DFS of the central phase part in
the proﬁle has a low-frequency modulation feature, with the
centroid frequency of 1/P3 = 0.032 ± 0.002, corresponding
to P3 = 31 ± 2, that requires longer observations to conﬁrm.

drifting mode is deﬁned as the Sd mode. Fluctuation spec-

4.303. J1945+2410g
PSR J1945+2410g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210707 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 2.3774 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 480.9 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 772 and ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 773 il-
lustrate that subpulses corresponding to leading and trailing
components all have negative drifting with similar tempo-
ral modulation periodicities. In 2DFS of the leading com-
ponent, the centroid frequencies of the negative drift feature
are 1/P3 = 0.085 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −22 ± 3, which cor-
respond to P3 = 11.7 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −16 ± 2◦.
2DFS of the trailing component exhibits a drift feature with
the centroid characterized by 1/P3 = 0.089 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −36 ± 6, yielding P3 = 11.3 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = −10 ± 2◦.

4.305. J1947+10
PSR J1947+10 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope at
430 MHz (Camilo et al. 1996a).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210806 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.1109 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 128.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences of the observation are shown in Fig. 777. The LRFS
and 2DFS for the leading, central, and trailing parts of the
mean pulse proﬁle are shown in Fig. 778, with three modu-
lation features corresponding to these proﬁle ranges. 2DFS
of the leading proﬁle part has a temporal modulation feature
with the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.427±0.002, corresponding to
P3 = 2.34±0.01 periods. In 2DFS of the central proﬁle part,
the preferred positive drift feature has centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.431±0.002 and 1/P2 = 18±8, yielding periodic-
ities of P3 = 2.32 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = 20 ± 10 periods.
There is a low-frequency modulation feature in 2DFS of the
trailing proﬁle part, while longer observations are required to
conﬁrm this feature.
4.306. J1951+1123
PSR J1951+1123 was discovered using the 305 m radio
telescope at Arecibo (Nice et al. 1995). Two drift features
were reported by Song et al. (2023), with temporal periodic-
ities of 4.8±0.2 and 17±3 periods.

PSR J1952+3021 was discovered in the PALFA survey us-
ing the Arecibo telescope (Patel et al. 2018). Nulling behav-
ior was displayed by (Parent et al. 2022) at 1.4 GHz from the

ergy histogram (Fig. 784). Fluctuation spectra are displayed

to P3 = 3.84 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = −6.1 ± 0.2 degrees.

PSR J1954+1021 was discovered in 350 MHz searches
with the Green Bank Telescope Kawash et al. (2018).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210117 for 9 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 2.0996 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 81.6 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence in Fig. 788 displays nulling and subpulse drifting
phenomena, which are reported for the ﬁrst time. The nulling
fraction is estimated to be 18±2% from the on-pulse inte-
gral energy histogram in Fig. 789. Fluctuation spectra shown
in Fig. 790 exhibit a negative drift feature, with the cen-
troid frequencies estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.3321 ± 0.004
and 1/P2 = −62 ± 2, corresponding to periodicities of
P3 = 3.012 ± 0.004 periods and P2 = −5.8 ± 0.2◦.
4.311. J1954+2529
PSR J1954+2529 was discovered in the Pulsar Arecibo L-
band Feed Array (PALFA) survey, which was reported to dis-
play nulling behavior (Parent et al. 2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220829 and
20230729 for 59 and 5 minutes, respectively.
From the
longer observation, a rotation period P = 0.9313 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 182.7 cm−3 pc were derived.
The single pulse sequence of the observation on 20230729
in Fig. 791 shows the nulling phenomenon, and the nulling
fraction is estimated from the on-pulse integral energy his-
togram (Fig. 792) to be 28.3±1.5%.
4.312. J1955+2930
PSR J1955+2930 was discovered by the Arecibo telescope
(Parent et al. 2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210315 for 7
minutes, with a rotation period and a dispersion measure
determined to be P = 1.0738 s and a dispersion measure
DM = 211.3 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences are shown
in Fig. 793, displaying the subpulse drifting phenomenon for
the trailing part in a mean pulse proﬁle. From the ﬂuctua-
tion spectra in Fig. 794, centroid frequencies of the negative
drift feature for the trailing proﬁle part are estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.200±0.002 and 1/P2 = −25±3, corresponding to
periodicities of P3 = 5.00 ± 0.05 periods and P2 = −15 ± 2
degrees.

Figure 796. Mean polarization proﬁles (the top panel) for weak and bright

sion measure DM = 153.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence in Fig. 795 displays mode changes between weak and
bright emission modes, which are labeled by red and green
colors, and there is also bi-drifting behavior for pulses No.
165-280 in the weak state. Mean polarization proﬁles and
the average PA curves of two emission modes are shown in
Fig. 796. The leading proﬁle part is stronger than the trail-
ing part for two modes, and the contrast between the two
proﬁle parts is much greater in the bright emission mode.
The difference between the PA curves of the two emission
modes over longitudes from −1.5◦to 1◦may be attributed
to the orthogonal modes. For pulses No. 165-280 of the
weak emission state, LRFS and 2DFS are shown in Fig. 797.
2DFS of the leading proﬁle part exhibits a negative drift fea-
ture, with the centroid modulation frequencies estimated to
be 1/P3 = 0.055 ± 0.004 and 1/P2 = −26 ± 10, cor-
responding to periodicities of P3 = 18 ± 1 periods and
P2 = −14 ± 5 degrees. For the trailing proﬁle part, the
centroid of the positive drift feature is characterized by fre-
quencies of 1/P3 = 0.056 ± 0.005 and 1/P2 = 27 ± 7,
yielding P3 = 18 ± 1 periods and P2 = 13 ± 4 degrees.
Detailed single pulse properties require more observations.
4.314. J1959+3036g
PSR J1959+3036g was discovered by Han et al. (2021)
from the FAST GPPS survey.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20230613 for 15
minutes, yielding a rotation period P = 2.8883 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 179.1 cm−3 pc. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 798 show the nulling phenomenon. The
on-pulse integral energy histogram is displayed in Fig. 799,
where the distribution around 0 energy conﬁrms the existence
of the nulling phenomenon. The nulling fraction of this ob-
servation is estimated to be 20.5±1.6%.

PSR J1959+3141g was discovered in the FAST GPPS sur-
vey (Han et al. 2021, 2025).

## Page 270


270
sequences in Fig. 806 show nulling and subpulse modula-
tion. The nulling fraction of this observation is estimated
to be 12.1±0.9% from the on-pulse energy histogram in
Fig. 807. From the ﬂuctuation spectra shown in Fig. 808, the
leading edge of the proﬁle exhibits a subpulse modulation,
while the central and trailing proﬁle parts both have nega-
tive subpulse drifting. For the leading edge of the proﬁle,
the centroid frequency of the modulation feature in 2DFS is
1/P3 = 0.395 ± 0.001, corresponding to P3 = 2.53 ± 0.01
periods. In the 2DFS of the central proﬁle part, the nega-
tive drift feature has a centroid at 1/P3 = 0.393 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −95 ± 4, yielding P3 = 2.55 ± 0.01 peri-
ods and P2 = −3.8 ± 0.2 degrees. For the trailing proﬁle
part, the negative drift feature in the 2DFS exhibits a cen-
troid at 1/P3 = 0.397±0.002 and 1/P2 = −26±5, yielding
P3 = 2.52 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = −14 ± 3 degrees.

4.320. J2003+2916
PSR J2003+2916 was discovered in the Pulsar Arecibo L-
band Feed Array (PALFA) survey (Parent et al. 2022).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211202 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.0100 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 208.0 cm−3 pc. Single pulse sequences
in Fig. 811 display a subpulse modulation with a periodicity
of about 2 periods, and two single pulses (Nos. 173 and 174)
have energies less than 3 σE. Fluctuation spectra are shown
in Fig. 813. In 2DFS of the leading proﬁle part, the nega-
tive drift feature has a centroid at 1/P3 = 0.462 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −19 ± 3, corresponding to P3 = 2.165 ± 0.004 pe-
riods and P2 = −18 ± 3◦. The trailing proﬁle part exhibits a
positive drift feature in 2DFS, and the centroid is character-
ized by 1/P3 = 0.455 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 16 ± 5, yielding
P3 = 2.20 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = 23 ± 8◦.
4.321. J2006+22
PSR J2006+22 was discovered in the LOFAR Tied-Array
All-Sky Survey (LOTAAS) (Sanidas et al. 2019).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220502 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 1.7417 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 130.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence (Fig. 814) and the on-pulse integral energy histogram
(Fig. 815) illustrate the nulling phenomenon. The nulling
fraction of this observation is estimated to be 29.3±3.6%.
4.322. J2006+4058
PSR J2006+4058 was discovered by the Five-hundred-
metre Aperture Spherical radio-Telescope (FAST) during its
commissioning (Cruces et al. 2021).
The pulsar was also observed by FAST on 20210324 for
5 minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.4997 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 257.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 816. In the 2DFS (Fig. 817), there
is a negative drift feature, with the centroid frequencies esti-
mated to be 1/P3 = 0.311±0.001 and 1/P2 = −21±2, cor-
responding to P3 = 3.22±0.01 periods and P2 = −17±1◦.

4.323. J2009+3326
PSR J2009+3326 was discovered by (Cordes et al. 2006)
using the Arecibo telescope.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200125 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.4384 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 264.8 cm−3 pc.
The single
pulse sequence in Fig. 818 displays negative subpulse drift-

4.327. J2016+1948
PSR J2016+1948 was discovered by the Arecibo Radio
Telescope (Navarro et al. 2003).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201110 for 8 min-
utes, and a rotation period P = 0.06495 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 33.8 cm−3 pc were determined. The single
pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view are shown in Fig. 827.
A decrease in energy for pulses 1385 to 1395 is revealed.
From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 828, the 2DFS of the trailing
proﬁle part exhibits a feature with the centroid frequencies of
1/P3 = 0.257 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = 10 ± 2, corresponding to
periodicities of P3 = 3.89 ± 0.03 periods and P2 = 37 ± 9
degrees. More FAST observations are required for the con-
ﬁrmation of whether the energy decrease corresponds to the
nulling state or the weak emission mode, as well as for a de-
tailed analysis.

minutes and 20241026 for 15 minutes. From the 15-minute

Figure 824. Fluctuation analysis of PSR J2011+3521g for the observation
on 20230804, with LRFS and 2DFS for the on-pulse region of a mean pulse
proﬁle.
PSR J2022+5154 was discovered by the Jodrell Bank Mk
I telescope (Davies & Large 1970). Subpulse drifting with
variable drift rate has been presented by Oster et al. (1977) at
1720 MHz and (Weltevrede et al. 2006) at 21 cm. Chen et al.
(2024) reported a 5-period subpulse drifting and a 40-period
periodic amplitude modulation at 2250 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20220917 for 84
minutes, driving a rotation period P = 0.5292 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 22.9 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 1-300 in Fig. 832
reveal the unsystematic subpulse drifting behavior. Fluctu-
ation spectra are shown in Fig. 833, in which the leading
and trailing parts of the mean pulse proﬁle exhibit differ-
ent modulation features.
For the leading proﬁle part, the
low-frequency modulation feature is more prominent than
the positive drift feature in 2DFS. The low-frequency fea-
ture has a centroid frequency of 0.0272 ± 0.0003 (P3 =
36.7±0.4 periods), and the drift feature is centered at 1/P3 =
0.172 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = 16 ± 1 (P3 = 5.80 ± 0.02 peri-
ods and P2 = 22 ± 1 degrees). For the trailing proﬁle part,
the centroid of the low-frequency modulation feature is at
1/P3 = 0.0529±0.0004 (P3 = 18.9±0.1 periods), and that
of the positive drift feature is at 1/P3 = 0.1708±0.0004 and
1/P2 = 22 ± 1 (P3 = 5.85 ± 0.01 periods and P2 = 16 ± 1
degrees).

PSR J2037+3621 was discovered in the Princeton-NRAO

Figure 858. Single pulse sequence of PSR J2045+3633 from the FAST

observation, a rotation period P = 0.0317 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 129.6 cm−3 pc were determined. Sin-
gle pulse sequences of the data on 20210708 are shown in
Fig. 858. This pulsar is found to have modulation behav-
ior from the ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 859, and the temporal
modulation periodicities are different for different longitude
regions corresponding to different components. The mod-
ulation frequencies on time of three longitude parts in the
proﬁle are widely distributed in 2DFS. In 2DFS of the lead-
ing proﬁle part, there seems a feature with the centroid mod-
ulation frequency of 1/P3 = 0.307 ± 0.001, correspond-
ing to P3 = 3.26 ± 0.01, as well as a low-frequency fea-
ture. However, the central and trailing longitude ranges have
different properties with the leading range. For the central
proﬁle part, the centroid temporal modulation frequency is
1/P3 = 0.120±0.001, yielding P3 = 8.35±0.05 periods. In
2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part, there is a subpulse drift fea-
ture with the centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.1172±0.0003
(P3 = 8.54 ± 0.02 periods) and 1/P2 = 11 ± 1 (P2 =
33 ± 2◦).

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250217 for 11
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 1.1383 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 39.7 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences in Fig. 860 show that both the leading and trail-
ing parts of the mean pulse proﬁle exhibit negative drifting
with variable drifting rates. Fluctuation spectra are shown in
Fig. 861, and drift features appear temporally broad, reﬂect-
ing variable drifting behavior. In 2DFS of the leading proﬁle
part, the drift feature has a centroid at 1/P3 = 0.049 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −38 ± 3, corresponding to P3 = 20.2 ± 0.4
periods and P2 = −10 ± 1 degrees. The centroid of the drift
feature is at 1/P3 = 0.080 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −56 ± 2,
yielding P3 = 12.5 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −6.5 ± 0.2
degrees.
4.342. J2048+4951

survey using the L-band receiver of the Nançay Radio Tele-
scope (Desvignes et al. 2022).

minutes and on 20250210 for 15 minutes.

minute data, a rotation period P = 0.5683 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 223.2 cm−3 pc were derived. Single
pulse sequences of the observation on 20250210 are shown
in Fig. 862, illustrating the existence of a temporal modula-
tion phenomenon. LRFS and 2DFS are shown in Fig. 863.

Figure 861. Fluctuation analysis of PSR J2046+1540 from the FAST ob-
servation on 20250217, with LRFS (top-left), and 2DFS for the on-pulse
region (top-right), leading part (bottom-left) and trailing part (bottom-right)
of the mean pulse proﬁle.
2DFS of the leading part in the mean pulse proﬁle has a
temporal modulation feature with the centroid frequency of
1/P3 = 0.085 ± 0.002, corresponding to P3 = 11.8 ± 0.2
periods. In the 2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part, the negative
drift feature exhibits the centroid of 1/P3 = 0.097 ± 0.001
and 1/P2 = −20 ± 4, yielding P3 = 10.3 ± 0.1 periods and
P2 = −18 ± 4◦. Single-pulse features of the observation on
20240731 are consistent with those on 20250210.

Figure 862. Single pulse sequences of PSR J2048+4951 from the FAST

PSR J2053+4718 was discovered by FAST in the Com-
mensal Radio Astronomy FAST Survey (CRAFTS) (Cruces
et al. 2021).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200810 for 15
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 4.9102 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 322.3 cm−3 pc were determined.
Single pulse sequences in Fig. 864 display nulling and pos-
itive subpulse drifting behaviors.
The nulling fraction of
this observation is estimated to be 53±2% from the on-
pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 865). Drifting param-
eters are obtained from the correlation method, which are
D = 0.65 ± 0.02 degrees per period and P2 = 2.04 ± 0.01◦.
4.344. J2055+3630
PSR J2055+3630 was discovered by the transit telescope
of the National Radio Astronomy Observatory (Damashek
et al. 1982). The fraction of null pulses was estimated to be
less than 0.7%, listed by Weisberg et al. (1986) at 430 MHz.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210621 for 5 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 0.2215 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 97.4 cm−3 pc. From single pulse
sequences and on-pulse integral energy histogram (Fig. 867
and Fig. 868), no nulling behavior exists in our observation.
In 2DFS (Fig. 869), there are broad drift features with a
preferred positive drift sense, and the wide modulation fre-
quency on phase is around zero, similar to the results of

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240731 for 5 min-
utes and 20240920 for 15 minutes, with a rotation period
of 0.2544 s and a dispersion measurement of 157.2 cm−3pc
from the observation on 20240920. Single pulse sequences
and ﬂuctuation spectra of the observation on 20240920 are
shown in Fig. 870 and 871, respectively. There is a positive
drift feature in 2DFS, with the centroid frequencies estimated
to be 1/P3 = 0.089±0.001 and 1/P2 = 8±2, corresponding
to periodicities of P3 = 11.3±0.1 periods and P2 = 43±9◦.
The single pulse behavior of the observation on 20240731 is
consistent with that on 20240920.

4.347. J2105+07
PSR J2105+07 was discovered in the Arecibo 327 MHz
Drift Pulsar Survey (Deneva et al. 2016).
The FAST observed the pulsar on 20210118 for 9 min-
utes, yielding a rotation period P = 3.7468 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 50.0 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence is shown in Fig. 874.
From the on-pulse inte-
gral energy histogram in Fig. 875, the nulling fraction of
this observation is ﬁtted to be 20±4%.
LRFS and 2DFS
of the trailing part in the mean pulse proﬁle are shown in
Fig. 876. 2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part exhibits a positive
drift feature, with the centroid frequencies estimated to be
1/P3 = 0.153 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = 34 ± 7, corresponding to
periodicities of P3 = 6.5 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 11 ± 2◦.

4.348. J2108+4441
PSR J2108+4441 was discovered by Davies et al. (1973)
using the Mark 1A radio telescope at Jodrell Bank. Low
frequency modulation has been detected (Weltevrede et al.
2006, 2007).
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20200308 for 5
minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.4149 s and a
dispersion measure DM = 140.1 cm−3 pc.
The single
pulse sequence in Fig. 877 illustrates that the drifting be-
havior is not systematic. From the 2DFS of the FAST ob-
servation in Fig. 878, there are mainly two drift features.

quence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 100-300 are
shown in Fig. 882, exhibiting no systematic drifting behav-
ior.
From ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 883, there is a pre-
ferred negative drift feature, with the temporal ﬂuctuation
frequency widely distributed. The centroid frequencies of
the drift feature are estimated to be 1/P3 = 0.232 ± 0.004
and 1/P2 = −13 ± 2, corresponding to periodicities of
P3 = 4.3 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = −28 ± 5 degrees.
4.351. J2116+3701
PSR J2116+3701 was discovered by the CHIME telescope
(Dong et al. 2023).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240720 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.1459 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 44.1 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are shown in Fig. 884, illustrating the existence of
nulls. The nulling fraction is estimated from the on-pulse
integral energy histogram in Fig. 885, which is 67±3%.
Interestingly, the pulse drifts from the later to the earlier
phase during the emission state and displays the negative
drifting phenomenon. Fluctuation spectra are displayed in
Fig. 886, where the centroid frequencies of the drift feature
are 1/P3 = 0.0055 ± 0.0001 and 1/P2 = −10 ± 1, cor-
responding to periodicities of P3 = 183 ± 5 periods and
P2 = −38±5◦. P3 = 181±15 periods and P2 = −38±17◦.
The temporally modulated periodicity of 181 periods is from
the occurrences of nulls.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240731 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 5.8012 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 259.7 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence in Fig. 891 displays the nulling phenomenon. The
nulling fraction of this FAST observation is estimated from
the on-pulse integral energy histogram to be 35±3%.
4.355. J2138+4911
PSR J2138+4911 was discovered by the Green Bank Tele-
scope (GBT) at 350 MHz (Hessels et al. 2008).
The FAST observed the pulsar on 20200402 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.6962 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 167.8 cm−3 pc. Single pulse se-
quences are displayed in Fig. 893. Fluctuation spectra in
Fig. 894 illustrate that the pulsar has a negative drifting be-
havior, and the range of the temporally modulated frequency
is wide. The centroid of the drift feature is characterized by
1/P3 = 0.101 ± 0.002 and 1/P2 = −24 ± 1, corresponding
to periodicities of P3 = 9.9±0.2 periods and P2 = −15±1◦.
4.356. J2150+5247
PSR J2150+5247 was discovered in the Princeton-NRAO
pulsar survey, carried out at 390 MHz using the 92 m tele-
scope at Green Bank (Dewey et al. 1985). Ng et al. (2020)
reported the discovery of the mode-switching behavior with
CHIME.

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20221120 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.3322 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 148.8 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view are displayed in Fig. 895.

periods). The centroid temporally modulated frequency re-
lated to the central proﬁle part in a mean proﬁle is 1/P3 =

4.358. J2208+4610
PSR J2208+4610 was discovered by Dong et al. (2023)
using the CHIME telescope.
The pulsar was observed by FAST on 20211124 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.6426 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 62.1 cm−3 pc. The single pulse se-
quence in Fig. 899 and ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 900 illus-
trate that the pulsar has subpulse drifting behavior, and the
leading part in the mean pulse proﬁle has a more system-
atic drifting than the trailing part. The centroid frequencies
of the drift feature in 2DFS of the leading proﬁle part are
1/P3 = 0.145 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = 15 ± 4, corresponding
to P3 = 6.9 ± 0.1 periods and P2 = 24 ± 6◦. For 2DFS
of the trailing proﬁle part, the drift feature exhibits centroid
frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.146 ± 0.003 and 1/P2 = 43 ± 5,
yielding P3 = 6.8 ± 0.2 periods and P2 = 8.4 ± 0.9◦.

PSR J2209+22 was reported as RRAT in the Pushchino
survey (Tyul’bashev et al. 2018a). This pulsar was detected
by FAST from the observation on 20210129 for 9 minutes,

4.361. J2219+4754
PSR J2219+4754 was discovered by Taylor & Huguenin
(1969) with the 300-foot transit telescope at the US National
Radio Astronomy Observatory. Weltevrede et al. (2006) re-
ported two vertical bands over the whole P3 range in 2DFS
observed at 21 cm. A preferred negative drift with P3 =
4.1 ± 0.6 periods and P2 = −140+10
−100 degrees was detected
by Weltevrede et al. (2007) at 92 cm, and the modulation of
this pulsar was interpreted as a quasi-periodic intensity mod-
ulation in the pulses, but not much correlation between the
subpulse positions from pulse to pulse.
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20210608 for 5 min-
utes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.5384 s and a dis-
persion measure DM = 43.3 cm−3 pc. The single pulse
sequence and a zoomed-in view of pulses No. 150-300 in
Fig. 905 show that most single pulses consist of several sub-

## Page 302

This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20240723 for 15
minutes and 20240908 for 14 minutes. From the data on
20240723, a rotation period P = 1.0305 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 125.3 cm−3 pc were derived. Single pulse
sequences of this observation are displayed in Fig. 907. From
ﬂuctuation spectra in Fig. 908, there are two drift features for
both components, corresponding to two drifting modes. The
modes with larger and smaller P3 are referred to as drift-
ing modes A and B, and their drifting parameters are derived
from the centroid in 2DFS. For the drifting mode A, the drift
feature of the leading proﬁle part has centroid frequencies
of 1/P3 = 0.141 ± 0.002 (P3 = 7.1 ± 0.1 periods) and
1/P2 = −43 ± 5 (P2 = −8 ± 1◦), and the trailing proﬁle
part has 1/P3 = 0.141 ± 0.002 (P3 = 7.1 ± 0.1 periods)
and 1/P2 = −32 ± 5 (P2 = −11 ± 2◦). For the drift-
ing mode B, the centroid frequencies of the feature in 2DFS
are 1/P3 = 0.292 ± 0.002 (P3 = 3.43 ± 0.02 periods) and
1/P2 = −66 ± 6 (P2 = −5.5 ± 0.5◦) for the leading proﬁle
part, and 1/P3 = 0.288 ± 0.001 (P3 = 3.48 ± 0.02 periods)
and 1/P2 = −41 ± 6 (P2 = −9 ± 1◦) for the trailing part.
Drifting parameters show large uncertainty and variations be-
tween two observations (See Table 4), likely attributable to
weak drift features in 2DFS. Accurate parameter determina-
tion requires longer observations.
4.363. J2329+4743
PSR J2329+4743 was discovered in the LOFAR Tied-
Array All-Sky Survey (LOTAAS) (Sanidas et al. 2019). The
pulsar was observed by FAST on 20201028 for 8 minutes,
yielding a rotation period P = 0.7285 s and a dispersion
measure DM = 43.4 cm−3 pc. There are systematic drift
bands in single pulse sequences (Fig. 909). LRFS and 2DFS
in Fig. 910 show a clear drift feature, and the range of tem-
porally modulated frequency is narrow, from which the lead-
ing and central parts in the mean pulse proﬁle have similar
temporal modulation periodicities. In 2DFS of the leading
proﬁle part, the drift feature has the centroid of 1/P3 =
0.218 ± 0.001 and 1/P2 = −14 ± 2, corresponding to peri-
odicities of P3 = 4.56 ± 0.02 periods and P2 = −26 ± 4◦.
2DFS of the central proﬁle part exhibits the drift feature,
with centroid frequencies of 1/P3 = 0.223 ± 0.001 and
1/P2 = −16 ± 3, yielding P3 = 4.48 ± 0.01 periods and
P2 = −22 ± 5◦.
4.364. J2338+4818
PSR J2338+4818 was discovered by FAST (Cruces et al.
2021).
This pulsar was also observed by FAST on 20250225 for
10 minutes, deriving a rotation period P = 0.1187 s and
a dispersion measure DM = 33.8 cm−3 pc.
The single
pulse sequence and a zoomed-in view are shown in Fig. 911.
From LRFS and 2DFS in Fig. 912, the trailing part in a mean
pulse proﬁle has a temporal modulation, with the centroid
frequency of 1/P3 = 0.400 ± 0.001, corresponding to the
periodicity of P3 = 2.50 ± 0.01 periods.

4.366. J2346-0609
PSR J2346-0609 was discovered by Manchester et al.
(1996) in the Parkes Southern Pulsar Survey. Previous stud-
ies have reported the low-frequency modulation, especially
in the leading component, as well as the subpulse drifting be-
havior of P3 ∼2.3 periods for the trailing component (Wel-
tevrede et al. 2006; Song et al. 2023). In addition, the pulsar
also has the nulling phenomenon, with a nulling fraction of
42.5±3.8% at 333 MHz and 28.7±1.8% at 618 MHz (Basu
et al. 2017).
This pulsar was observed by FAST on 20250223 for 11
minutes, with a rotation period P = 1.1815 s and a disper-
sion measure DM = 22.9 cm−3 pc determined. Single pulse
sequences in Fig. 915 illustrate that the pulsar has nulling,

component. For the PA curves of the two modes, there is

the strong low-frequency modulation feature in the 2DFS of

2DFS of the trailing proﬁle part exhibits a modulation fea-
ture with the centroid temporal modulation frequency of
1/P3 = 0.448 ± 0.002, corresponding to the periodicity of
P3 = 2.23 ± 0.01 periods.
5. DISCUSSION AND CONCLUSIONS
Using FAST single pulse time-series data obtained via the
GPPS survey and other projects by the authors, we detect the
nulling from 160 pulsars, mode changing from 52 pulsars,
and subpulse modulation from 272 pulsars. Among them,
these phenomena from 127, 51 and 180 pulsars are newly de-
tected, respectively. This substantially expands the samples
of pulsars with these single pulse behaviors.
As we see above, some pulsars exhibit two or more of these
features, for example, mode changes accompanied by dis-
tinct subpulse driftings, the nulling with mode changing. For
some pulsars with a similar P3, such as PSRs J1940+0239,
J1954+2923 and J2051+4434g, the drifting bands may be
discontinuous across different longitude ranges, which may
be related to phase-locked drifting and phase steps for dif-
ferent components (Bhattacharyya et al. 2009; Zhang et al.
2019).
Pulsars exhibiting single-pulse behaviors are investigated
through two kinds of statistic analyses: the interplay with
pulsar fundamental parameters – rotation period (P), pe-
riod derivation ( ˙P), magnetic ﬁeld strength (B = 3.2 ×
1019G
√
P ˙P), characteristic age (τ = P/(2 ˙P)) or spin-
down energy loss ( ˙E ≃3.95 × 1031erg/s(
˙P
10−15 )( P
s )−3)
via histogram analyses, the correlations between these fun-
damental parameters and drifting subpulse properties, mainly
the time interval P3 and phase separation P2 between drifting
bands. One can see from the P −˙P diagram in Figure 920 for
known pulsars that pulsars exhibiting nulling are more con-
centrated to the death line (Ruderman & Sutherland 1975).
Histograms of rotation period P, period derivation
˙P,
magnetic ﬁeld strength B, characteristic age τ or spin-down
energy loss ˙E for pulsars with nulling, mode-changing, and
subpulse modulation are shown in Fig 921.
To compare
whether the parameters of pulsars exhibiting these phenom-
ena and those of all known normal pulsars (with P >50 ms)
originate from the same distributions, Kolmogorov-Smirnov
tests (Hodges 1958) are performed. Parameters of all known
normal pulsars are taken from the ATNF pulsar catalog
(Manchester et al. 2005), the most complete sample of known
pulsars currently available. The null hypothesis states that
both samples originate from the same distribution, and that
the KS statistic is large and the p-value is less than 0.05 in-
dicates that the distributions are statistically incompatible.
From histograms in Figure 921 and statistic values and p-
values of K-S tests are listed in Table 6.
For pulsars with nulling phenomenon, the p-values corre-
sponding to P, τ, B and ˙E are less than 0.05, and as a result,
the null hypothesis is rejected. This means that these param-
eters of nulling pulsars with those of all known pulsars are
from distinct distributions. Combined with analysis of dis-
tributions in Figure 921, pulsars exhibiting nulling behavior
are older and have longer periods, stronger magnetic ﬁeld
strengths, and lower spin-down energy loss, which agrees
with previous studies (Ritchings 1976; Wang et al. 2007; Ng
et al. 2020).
Mode-changing pulsars were reported to have longer pe-
riod (Ng et al. 2020). While p-values about all pulsar parame-
ters corresponding to mode-changing behavior in Table 6 are
all more than 0.05. As a result, we cannot reject the null hy-
pothesis that the parameters of pulsars with mode-changing
phenomenon are from different distributions with that of all
known pulsars.
Similarly, the quantities of drifting pulsars, including pe-
riod, period derivation, characteristic age, and spin-down en-
ergy loss, are from different distributions with that of all
known pulsars with low p-values. Therefore, in addition to
older age as Weltevrede et al. (2006) reported, pulsars with
subpulse drifting behavior also tend to have longer period,
lower period deviation, and spin-down energy loss.
In conclusion, pulsars with nulling and subpulse modula-
tion behaviors tend to have longer periods, older ages, and
lower spin-down energy loss. Additionally, pulsars exhibit-
ing subpulse modulation phenomena have lower period devi-
ation, while those showing nulls tend to have stronger mag-
netic ﬁeld strength.
To measure the correlation between drifting parameters P3
and P2 with the pulsar parameters for pulsars with the ro-
tation period more than 50 ms, Spearman correlation coefﬁ-
cients and associated p-values (Zwillinger & Kokoska 2000;
Kendall & Stuart 1979) are calculated and listed in Table 6,
with the null hypothesis that two quantities are uncorrelated.
Spearman correlation coefﬁcients of -1 or +1 imply an ex-
act negatively or positively monotonic relationship, and a p-
value less than 0.05 indicates that two quantities are corre-
lated.
From Figure 922 and Table 6, the correlation coefﬁcients
are relatively higher, and the p-values are far less than 0.05
for correlations of P3 with P, ˙P, and B, which manifests
that P3 has higher negative correlations with rotation period,
period derivation, and magnetic ﬁeld strength.
However,
smaller correlation coefﬁcients and higher p-values more
than 0.1 of P3 with pulsar period derivation ˙P and charac-
teristic age τ cannot indicate the correlations. P2 in degrees
is negatively correlated with rotation period P, period deriva-
tion ˙P and magnetic ﬁeld strength B. However, P2 in mil-
lisecond units is positively correlated with rotation period P,
and negatively correlated with spin-down energy loss ˙E. This
is different from the correlations when P2 is expressed in de-
grees.
